was thought that it might prove of interest to the Department se

Heavy Indastries, Limited. As is indicated in the contract, the
abeve-mamed Uaion is recogaised as the enly union at the Tope
Engineering Works plant and the Company agrees to have nothing to
do with any other labor organization. By this contract, the Cempamy
agrees to pay a wage that will stabilize the cost of living of the
wmiom-member workers. Provision is made for an eight-hour working
day with a one-hour rest period. Article 4 is of particular interest
since the Company cannct unilaterally employ, discharge, ehange er
promote persennel, or, raise wages, make rewards or institute
pemalties. In this connection, the Company has indicated that an
understanding has been reached with the Union whereby the Umion agrees
in general mot to interfere in changes of chiefs and heads of sectiens;
however, the Company will inform the Union in advance of proposed
changes and will take imto consideration Union wishes in this regard.
According to the contract, the Company agrees to discharge workers
expelled by the Union, and further, that it shall not contract with
other suppliers of labor to carry on work during a labor dispute.
Provision is made for the conclusion of a separate contract
between the Gompany and Union to establish a Mamagement-Labor Conference
to carry out the provisions of the labor contract, and matters agreed
upon by both are to be contractually binding. Article 9 is alse
worthy of special note since it provides that the Company camnot
close the plant for any appreciable length of time, mor can it
change the mame of the plant, or transfer any land, buildings,
equipment, machines or other Company property without first giving
notice to the Union. The Union is to participate im the promotion
of welfare activities and must consent te steps taken in this regard.
The Company is prohibited from revising er abolishing plant regulations
or systems of allowances without Union consent. With respect to
labor movements, Union members have complete freedon of actien and
the Company may not discrimimate in any way against them. Officials
of the Union are free to devote themselves to Union activities;
however, it is stipulated that the Company should be infermed of this
in advance. Likewise, Union members are free to join any political
party or to hold public office without interference from the Company.

POLICIES TO COPE WITH THE IMPENDING
ECOMOMIC COLLAPSE OF JAPAN
Japan is teday on the verge ef ecomomic collapse, and iaflation
is about to assume a calamitous proportion. Mamufasturing and
miming outputs are at the low point of about 30% the pre-war levels.
The official commedity prices have climbed to 40 times the pre-war
figures, accerding to the Bank of Japan index of Tokyo retail priees.
Rice, the staple feod and the biggest single item in the cest of
living of the Japanese people, is nov officially 67 timesas expensive
as before the hostilities, and in the black market has soared to the
astounding height of 580 times the pre-war price.
The Bank of Japan note issue, never more than *l, 600 milliem
before 1941, has passed the epochal 3200 billion mark at the end
of last year. Specially nete-worthy in this commectien is the fact
that, since April, l947, commodity prices have registered a far
sharper advance than the increase in the volume ef currency. This
resulted in an acute shortage of money for all institutioms, public
and private. Large-scale releases of financial and industrial funds
are demanded in conseyuence, aggravating the already dangerous
inflation.
The contiaued coal shortage and the recent insufficiency of
electric power supply have combined to cut down severely the out-
puts of iron and steel and other essential productive materials.
The very small preduction and the depleting stock-piles of these
materials are adversely affecting all industries, and are bound
to accelerate the inflationary tendency. These tegether with the
unsound program of public fimance and thoughtless ways ef cellecting
taxes and making payments led Japan face to face with a runasay
inflation. Short of very drastic measures taken immediately to
cembat this very real peril, mot omly is the ecomomic reconstruction
of the ceuntry absolutely hopeless, providing hot bed for the
further imfiltratioa of Cemmumism, but the execution of the eccu-
pation policies will be in danger of being serioualy hampered with.
As the drastic measures meeded to cope with this situation,
we venture to suggest the following:
1. Laber Uprest and Trepbles
Of the mumerous causes impeding the ecomomic recevery of Japan
teday, such as the shortages of capital, materials, foodstuffs and
ether necessities of life, the most serious is undoubtedly labor
unrest permeating all places of work and the commumist-directed
acts to ebstract productive efforts of all sorts. Nerely te over-
come the bottlemecks of capital and material shortages would mot
accomplish increased productien se long as labor uarest and troubles
remain, creating as they do fresh bottlemecks in the supply ef raw
materials and transportation of the same.
An iastance to the point is the coal preductiem in Hokkaido.
However much capital, matorials, laber, foodstuffs and other
necessities

Labor unions under commumist domination are aware of the fact
that their gemeral strike tactics had to be abandened under the

people at large. They are mow resorting, with wave-like precision
and repetitien, to wildcat and local strikes. These attempts at
hampering productive activities, and threby bringing catastrephic
inflation, ecomomic chaes, and social disorder, are all after the
same Communist Internationale pattern ef actioms pursued every-
where. As long as this situation contimues, not ealy will the
domestic imvestors hesitate to put their capital imto enterprises.
but foreign capital canmot be induced to this country, mo matter
bew much we desire it to find its way here. It can be truthfully
said, therefere, that the reconstruction of Japan will mot be
achieved without first doing away with labor unrest and troubles.
For this end, we must advecate the elimination of communistic
elements and the amendment of labor laws.
(1) Elimimation of Communistic Elements.
a). Some actiens should be taken agaimst those whe imperil the

b). Measures should be adepted to drive communists and their
fellow travellers out of all laber umions and other fields
connected with productive activities.
(2). Amendment

II. Thorough-going Reorganization of Eaterprises.
Recomstruction of Japan's economy must be undertaken with the
object of participating in the near future in the ecomemy of the
werld. During the war years, increased productiem was attempted
merely through forced expansion of capital investment, in utter
disregard of the productivity of various industries and of the
intermational standard of technolegy. Over-expanded esterprises
should in these post-war days be thoroughly overhauled and completely
rationalised as a matter of course, to the end of preparing them to
have their parts eventually in the world ecomomy.
It is true that, imsofar as financial aspects are concermed,
the swollem capitalization was considerably reduced by the cancel-
lation of the government war indemnities. But mo imprevement has
so far takem place on the techaical side of productive efforts.
Productivity declined to a marked degree, due to the diminished
will to work caused by the existence of surplus labor which in
turn was resulted by the retura of demebilised and repatriated
- a surplus which cannot be reduced because of me called full
empleyment theory. The resultant high production cests threw
Japamese goods out of aligment with competing goods in ths marts
of the world. Basically high commodity prices in Japan have their
root in the lowered productivity of her industries.  Unless and
until this fundamental hamdicap is removed, the onslaught of in-
flation can never be checked.
In the face of impending runaway inflation, thorough-geing
reorganization of enterprises is urgently required.
III. Admimistrative Reforms.
We strongly insist upon the ratiomalisation of all gevernment
organs, be they engaged in state enterprises or im purely admimist-
rative functions, mnd that as a prelude to the rationalizatien of
private business. Two things cempel us to this position of ours.
One obvioualy is that squandering of public funds, inefficiency
and retention

and retention of superaumerary workers are the unsurmomtable im-
pediment to budgetary economy and an important contributery cause
of aggravating inflation. The other is the fact that by the capi-
tulatiom to the Government Railways Workers' Union, which lauached
an offensive under the slogan of "full employment", in the summer
of l946, the Govermment set a precedent in labor relatioms, and
paved the way for the incessant demands in all subsequent labor
disputes for "full empleyment" as if it was an indisputable secial
justice.
History proves that full employment is hard of achievement
even in countries of vast territories, unlimited resources, and
trememdous imdustrial capacities, at the most prosperous of times.
How much more difficult then it must be for the present-day Japan
to realise this ideal. Considerable unemploymemt will have to be
faced as inevitable because of the urgently needed reorganisatioms
within, and adjustments among, different industries. It goes
without saying that unemployment allowances, unemployment insurance,
and other social measures must be provided for the amelioration of
the let of the jobless. Yet it is a stern fact that, without
facing unemployment, Japan's industries can never hope to be
rationalized and put on a sound basis.
Marshalling all the powers at its command, therefore, the
Government should accomplish its administrative reforms, reduce the
surplus personnel, and so set an example for private busimess to
follow. As of October, l947, goverment officials, excluwsive of
those in state enterprises, reached the unprecedented number of
510,000, which is more than five times the total of govermment
workers in the days of budgetary economy of 1932. The Goverament
intends to effect a mere 20% personnel reduction in preparing its
1948 budget. To such a half measure we are unalterably opposed.
Iv. Determination of Yen-Dollar Standard Equivalence.
In the existing state of our foreign commerce, it would be
premature to fix a single rate of exchange between the dollar
and yen. We do hope, however, that at an early date a standard
of equivalence be fixed even temporarily between the two curren-
cies.
Care should be taken, when fixing such a standard, mot te
evaluate the yen at too low a level under the influence of indvidual
cases and instances. What we desire above all is to have a rational
equivalence determined that will be well-adapted and fit for the
general trend ef our future fereign trade. Once such an equivalence
is fixed, it is very much hoped that it will be actively supported
by the United States.
Fixation of equivalence will, as its corollary, bring about
the virtual extinction of enterprises that are over-staffed and
whose cost of production runs teo high. That is precisely what
it is expected to accomplish, vis., to accelerate and give direc-
tion for the rationalisation of industries. Without radical re-
organisations and readjustments begun in this fashion we shall
never be qualified, we believe, to ask for American credit, as we
constantly do, or to expect the inflow of foreign capital in the
future.

CONCLUSION
We feel that we have been highly privileged te have had this
oppertumity of candidly stating a fow fundamental measures deemed
essential to cope with the present ecomomic crisis, and of bring
ing them to your kind attention.
Most of these measures deal with problems of domestic ecomomy,
but in our opimion they are the conditions precedent to obtaiming
fereign credit. These measures we here advocate, because the
nation, unable to recover through her owm economic strength alene,
must, and we hope will, have a sizable oredit given her. It is
our comviction that omly by the grant of such a credit will our
country be able te rebuild her econemy, participate in the world
ecomomy when allowed to do so, and make her utmost contribution
to the mew economic order of the world.
The Gemeral Report of the Cemmittee on European Ecomomic Co-
operation says, in its General Outlime, that it is essential fer

experts that the American market should absorb them, and allow
•ther countries to acquire Americam dollars with which te pay for
their impurts. The Report imsists that there exists intimate re-
latioms between the problems ef the world economy and the European
recovery plan.
Thoroughly convinced that our economic reconstructiom is mo
less imtimately related te the problems of the werld ecomomy, we

a Harshall Plam fer the Far East new cemmands the attention of

Doed of Changinc Laber Pelier as the
Fundamental Preregnisite for
Boomomic Robabilitation of Japan
By
KITAOKA Juitsu
Former Chief of the Japanese Delegation
to the International Labour Organisation;
former professor of social policy at the
Tokyo Imperial University; and former
Chief of Labor Division of Economio
Stabilization Board.
I
Impertance of Labor in Japanese Economy
Japan is characterised by excessive population and poverty
of resources. She depends entirely or largely upon foreign
countries for the supply of such raw materials as cotton, woel,
iron ore, oil, fat, rubber, et cetera. Japan must also import
food and coal to some extent. Her dependence upon foreign countries
markedly increased after the war, because of the loss of overses
resources and a rise of population. Japan's export trade, indispen-
sable for the purchase of raw materials from abroad, depends upen
imported raw materials. The only asset for Japan to compete in
foreign marksts is her high labor efficiency in proportien to wages.
The best example is furaished by the cotton and other textile
industries. The raw silk industry - the only industry operated
with domestic material - is based upon industrious laber. Indeed,
labor may be called the only asset of Japan.
Productivity of labor, however, has been reduced to one-third
after the war. This is the principal cause of industrial decay in
this country.
II
Labor Lawe go too far, and are abused
by Labor Unions
l. The Labor Union Law, in Article ll, prohibits the employer with
penalty from dismissing or discriminating an employee because
of his or her being a union member or legitimate union activity.
This provision is interpreted by labor unioms in an unduly
broad sense. All employees, so long as they are union members
(practically all employees are labor union members), do not
obey work orders and violate work regulations. And yet the
employer cannot discharge them, however low their efficiency
ratings may be. Efficiency rule which is the absolute require-
ment in factory management cannot be carried out. The workers'
right of strike is guaranteed to the maximum degree, while the
employers' right of lockout or discharge is denied. This is
the principal cause of industrial paralysis.
2. Article 2 of the Labor Union Law provides that the penal ccde
does not
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.97)
COMPIMTIAL (For Use of Department Only)
Page No. 2 to Emalesureo
2 to Despateh No. 95 dätéd
February 15, 1948, frem
Tokyo, Japan, entitled
"Comments Regarding the
Labor Situation in Japan".
-2-
does not apply to the workers for their legitimate activities
for attaining the purposes for which unions are established.
This provision is also interpreted in an unduly broad sease.
Net only are mass demonstrations staged and interviews with
directors forcibly requested by unions, but also violencs
and intimidation toward managing directors and their families
are publicly committed. And yet neither legal action mor
the right of discharge can be exercised. Interference of a
labor union with management is too great.
3. The staff members are also labor union members. This strengthens
labor unions all the more, and weakens the power of mamagement
proportionally. The labor union concludes a collective agreement
with management with a powerful organization behind. Deprived of
the powers of employmont, discharge and even of management by
labor unions, many industries became practically.paralysed.
4.
The labor umiom in Japan, which was given the right of unionisation
and strike unparalleledly high in the world without passing through
democratic training, has fallen into despotism exercised by a
minority group. As a large number of labor leaders are under
Communist influence, a large proportion ef labor unioas
(including unions of government workers and teachera) are under
Communist influence, in spite of the fact that union members are
citizens with common sense (as revealed by a small number of votes
cast for the Commumist Party).
The employer has no right of discharging the employees when they
resort to sympathy strikes. Neither has the government power to
stop such acts. This has placed the unions of public services,
such as coal, electricity, railways, communications, et cetera,
on the equal footing with the Diet and the government. Since
these unioms are being ruled by a small number of Communists, mo
political stability can be maintained even under a fres and
democratic government.
That the Labor Standards Law goes too far and labor administration
does not fit the actual conditions cannot be overlooked. The
crux of the question, however, lies in the Labor Union Law.
Unless this law is revised, it is considered impossible to check
inflation and rehabilitate the Japanese economy.
III
How to Revise Labor Union Lay
1. The labor union should be democratised and Commumist dictator
ship rejected. The revenues of labor unions should be reportad
to the goverment for publication, with a view to preventing
fereign funds from pouring into unions.
3. Discharge ef the workers should be made possible whenever
they violate the work regulations and when their efficiency
ratinge are teo low.
3. The right ef the employer should be recognised to discharge
the workers

the workers who join sympathy strikes. Such acts ef the wrkers
as violence, intimidation, libel cases, personal sttasks en
direstors, assaults upon the private residences of directing
officials, et cetera, should be made illegal.
5.
The government should be empowered to stop general strikes
in public utilities, such as railways, coal, electricity, et
cetera.
With respect te malicious labor movements led'by Cemmunists,
the goverament should be empowered to suppress the Cemsemists,
and the employer to dismiss a worker who acts with Cemmumist
conviction. This will bring about the same objectives as
revising the Labor Union Law.

Enclosure to Despatch No. 151 dated
March 9, 1948 from Office of the United
States Political Adviser at Tokyo, om
the subject: "Tranamitting Copy of Poliey
Statement Regarding Application of Press
Code to Newspaper Labor Relations."
Source: GHQ, FEC, Public Information
Office, 1000, 3 March 1948.
Press Release:
APPLICATION OF PRESS CODE TO NEWSPAPER LABOR RELATIONS CLARIFIED
IN POLICY STATEMENT
Application of the Press Code to newspaper labor relations
was clarified today in a policy statement by the Labor Division
of SCAP's Economic and Scientific Section.
The statement makes these points:
1 - Stable and effective labor relations call for complete
acceptance by both employers and unions of the sole and complete
responsibility of management to determine editorial content in
the press and publication industries. If necessary, this clear
statement should be incorporated in all collective bargaining
agreements as soon as possible.
2 - Management has the right to take appropriate disciplinary
action against employees or union members in these industries who
attempt by use of force to determine in any way, directly or in-
directly, editorial policy or content, or who interfere with publi-
cation or distribution of printed matter.
3 - To prevent use of the Press Code by management as a pre-
taxt for discharge or other discrimination against employees, man-
agement should consult with the union before taking action. Cases
involving question of fact should be referred to an impartial arbi-
trator or third party.
4 - There is no intention of preventing or weakening existing
practices under which management-labor concurrence is needed in
discharging, disciplining, or rewarding employees not involved in
these specific issues, or of interfering with restrictions against
discriminatory discharges as set forth in Article ll of the Trade
Union Law.
A Labor Division official said the statement, drawn up in con-
sultation with Civil Information and Education Section officials,
incorporates an interpretation of the Press Code as given by Lt.
Col. Donald R. Nugent, CI&E chief, on June 13, 1946.
Colonel Nugent declared at that time that freedom of the press
included the right to present news and editorial opinion free from
influence, domination, or any other form of control by any govern-
ment agency, political party, employers' association, employees'
association, labor union or any other pressure group.
"SCAP officials hold editors or publishers responsible for the
content of their publications, making it impossible to allow trade
unions to bargain for the right to influence content of a publi-
cation, or to accomplish the same aim through dispute tactics," a
Labor Division official said.
He

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure to Tokyo's No. 151,
March 3, 1948.
-2 -
He recommended that disputes arising over Article Two of the
master contract for newspapers, which requires union consent to
discharge, transfer or demotion of personnel, be referred to local
labor relations committees.
"Committees should make their decisions within the meaning
and intent of the Press Code," the official said, adding that
questions on violation of the code itself must be determined by
occupation authorities.

Tokyo, March 25, 1948
PERSONAL and CONF IDENTIAL
Dear John:
As you are probably aware, the labor situation in Japan appears
to be in a chaotic condition. without attempting to go into the
underlying reasons for the present situation, suffice it to say that
one of the contributory causes is undoubtedly the policy which has
been pursued by the SCAP Labor Section, particularly during the first
year of the Occupation. As a result, we are now faced with a series
of "quickie" strikes on the part of a large number of Government
labor unions, a situation which might very well eventually get out
of hand and once again call for strong action on the part of General
MacArthur. It is barely possible, of course, that the present
894.504/
Cabinet will be able to control the recalcitrant unions, but as a
long-range proposition I can only foresee continued trouble.
In my opinion, the labor situation in Japan is closely allied
with the political situation; in fact, the latter can largely be a
result of labor's attitude towards any government which might be in
office. What might happen if the Occupation control should suddenly
be relaxed is anybody's guess although I believe with proper handling
and advice the situation is not beyond salvage.
As a result of the above, I consider it highly desirable that
the Department consider the possibility of assigning a Labor Attachè
to this Mission. The individual who is chosen should necessarily be
3
one who has the confidence of the Department, the Department of Labor,
as well as the CIO and AFL. I might say that the Labor Attachè need
not necessarily have had previous Japanese experience; in fact, he
2548
might be more valuable if he has never been in Japan.
For your information, I have not yet cleared this idea with any
one in Headquarters but Maynard will approach General Marquat with
the idea of having a labor man come out under the auspices of the
Department "with a view to making an objective study of the labor
situation". It is my idea thet the present people in the Labor
Division are so strongly entrenched and so sensitive of their position
that a frontal attack would hardly do. Once the Labor Attachè were
here, however, I think it would not be difficult to have him continue
his assignment indefinitely.
I suggest
John M. Allison, Esquire,
Chief, Division of Northeast Asian Affairs,
Department of State,
Washington 25, D.C.

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.93)
- 2 -
I suggest that no definite assignment be made until I have had
an opportunity to lay the groundwork here and until I send an
official request to the Department (I will see that you get an advance
copy of such request). In the meantime, you may wish to set the
search for the right individual in motion, as presumably it will take
some time to find just the proper man.
Sincerely yours,
Rll
W. J. Sebald

YOKOHAMA SHIP YARD & ENGINE WORKS
HITSUBISHI HEAVY INDUSTRIES, LTD.
3 Midoricho, Nishiku, Yokohama, Japan
LABOR AGREEMENT
November 1, 1947
The Yokohama Shipyard & Engine Works, Mitsubishi Heavy
Industries Ltd. (hereafter referred to as "the Company") and
the Salarymen's Union of the Mitsubishi Yokohama Shipyard
(hereafter referred to as "the Union"), have concluded this
Agreement with the ain of contributing to company's interest
by improving labor status together with the maintenance and
furtherance of efficiency with mutual understanding and
close cooperation between both parties, thereby promoting
national economy to wit:
Art. 1. T
The Company shall recognize the Union as a sole
independent society of salaryment in the Company
and shall carry on no negotiations outside of the
Union.
Art. 2.
Those being usually employed by the Company shall
necessarily be the members of the Union, provided
that those who fall under the provision of No. 1,
Art. 2 of the Labor Union Law and those who are
permitted by both the Company and the Union shall
not be included.
Art. 3.
The Company, conferring with the Branch, shall
establish a minimum wage system based upon the
living cost of the members of the Union conforming
to the fluctuation of prices.
Art. 4.
The following matters shall be negotiated and
decided at the Hanagement Council (hereafter referred
to as "the Council") composed of members repre-
senting the Company and the Union.
1. Matters as to the system of the Company.
2.
Basic matters of personal affairs such as
employment, discharge, appointment, promotion,
transfer of post, or revard and punishment.
3.
Matters of working condition such as payment,
allowances and wages, working time, and
holiday.
4.
Administrative matters of labor, materials,
technique, etc. in the course of production.
5.
Matters of culture and welfare such as educa-
tion, public welfare, health, fire prevention,
etc.
6.
Amendments of this Agreement.
7.
Other matters which the Company or the Union
deems necessary to negotiate.
Rules

Rules of the Council shall be fixed separately
under the joint deliberation of the Company and the
Union.
Art. 5.
The Company shall after management data necessary
to the Union, provided that the latter shall not
divulge any secret matter.
Art. 6.
The Company shall negotiate with the Union on
important administrative matters such as dissolu-
tion, suspension of operation, amalgamation, separa-
tion, change of title, transference or diversion
of the Company's assets, etc.
Art. 7.
In case that there should be any dispute between
the Company and the Union, it shall, by mutual
understanding, be brought to the Labor Committee
under the Labor Union Law for conciliation or
arbitration.
In the above case, should the Labor Board
conciliate or arbitrate, the Company or the Union
shall not start such measures as closing of shops,
general strikes, etc.
Art. 8.
The Company and the Union shall strictly abide by
the provisions of this Agreement and the matters
decided by the Management Council under Art. 4 of
this agreement.
Art. 9.
This Agreement shall remain in force for one (1)
year from the day of Agreement.
Art. 10.
In case of the establishment of second company,
this present Agreement shall remain in effect.

Art. 8.
The Council shall be convoked at the latest upon a
three-day notice of its agenda to its members;
however, provided that this rule shall not apply
in case of emergency.
Art. 9.
Without the consent of both the Company and the
Union the Council c annot convene.
Art. 10.
A chairman shall preside at the meeting of the
Council to manage the procedure of the meeting.
Art. 11.
The chairman shall be elected from among the
members of the Council. As to his appointment,
the agreement of both the Company and the Union
shall be required on the Ordinary Meeting; how-
ever, in case of an Extraordinary Meeting, the
chairman of the non-proposing party shall preside.
Art. 12.
The decision of the meeting shall be made by a
majority vote.
In case of a tie, a revote shall be cast.
Art. 13.
When a special investigation is necessary of
certain questions of the Council, a Special
Committee may be appointed to make the investiga-
tion.
Art. 14.
The details of the Special Meeting shall be fixed
in accordance vith the occasion.
Art. 15.
When necessary, the Council shall be able to
convene a joint meeting of vorkers and office
clerks through the negotiation of the Company and
the Union, provided that the vote shall be taken
of cach meeting separately.
Art. 16.
The matters discussed at the Council Heeting shall
be strictly confidential
Art. 17.
The minutes of the meetings of the Council shall
be recorded by the secretary.
Two copies shall be made of questions decided
upon with the signatures and seals of the repre-
sentatives of both parties and a copy is kept in
file by each party.
SUPPLEIENTARY REGULATIONS
Art. 18.
The revision of the above regulations shall be
enacted by the negotiation of the Council.
Art. 19.
The above regulations are effective as of November
1, 1947.

YOKOHAMA SHIPYARD & ENGINE WORKS
MITSUBISHI HEAVY INDUSTRIES, LTD.
3 Hidoricho, Nishiku, Yokohama, Japan
LABOR AGREEIENT
November 1, 1947
The Yokohana Shipyard & Engine Morks, Mitsubishi Heavy
Industries Ltd. (hereafter referred to as "the Company") and
All Japan Shipbuilding Workers' Union, the Mitsubishi Branch
have come to conclude the folloving Labor Agreement:
FUNDALMENTAL AGREEIENT:
Art. 1.
The Company shall recognize the All Japan Ship-
building Vorkers' Union and the Branch as an
autonomous society in the Company and shall carry
on all negotiations only with the Branch or the
said Union.
Art. 2.
The Company shall be able to employ a non-union
member only upon the recognition of the Branch.
VAGES:
Art. 3.
The Company, conferring with the Branch, shall
establish a minimum wage system based upon the
living ∞ st of the menbers of the Branch and their
families conforming to the fluctuation of prices.
Art. 4.
The Company shall enact amendment on wages and
allowances upon negotiation with the Branch.
WORKING HOURS:
Art. 5.
The standard of working hours shall be eight (8)
hours, with seven (7) actual working hours per
day.
Art. 6.
Whenever a work requires overtime or off day work
the Company shall negotiate with the Branch.
HOLIDAYS AND LEAVES OF ABSENCE:
Art. 7. Holidays and Leave of Absence shall be to wit:
1. Holidays
a) Sundays
b) National Holidays
2. Leave with Pay
a) Annual Leave (with pay)
For both men and women a leave of fourteen
(14) days will be granted per year (a
calendar

To those in continuous service from nine

Employees shall be relieved from duty on

head three days leave of absence with 60
percent of their average pay per day.

f) Food Procuration.
In case of ration distribution is delayed

of average wage per day.
g) Marriage.
Workers shall be relieved of duty for four
(4) days in the case of marriage, and they
shall be paid at the rate of 6o percent of
average wage per day.
h) Mourning.
The following leave of absence shall be
granted with daily pay in each case:
1) For parents, consorts, and children--
under seven'(7) days.
2) For brothers, sisters, or grandfathers
and grandmothers related by blood, or
parents of the consorts--under three (3)
days.
i) Before and After Childbirth.
Female workers shall have leave of absence
for six (6) weeks each before and after
childbirth. Upon request a pregnant female
worker will be transferred to a lighter
work.
Art. 8.
Employees shall be paid allowance for suspension
of work at the rate of 60 percent of average wage
in the following cases:
hen the conpany suspends work due to the lack
of work, shortage of materials or electricity,
etc.
2.
When transportation or communication is un-
available due to accidents and forbids employees
to report to work.
3。
When the company forbids employees to work due
to their condition of health.
4.
When shops ∞uld not be operated by the cur-
tailment of electricity.
5.
In the course of weather or nature of work
calls for an untinely attendance or dismissal
the di ference shall be covered by the above
allowance.
INJURY AND SICKNESS WIILE OH DUTY
Art. 9.
In case a member of the Branch has been injured or
taken sick due on duty the Company shall pay the
while expenses for medical treatment and 100
percent of his daily wage.

Art. 10.
The Company shall hold periodically medical exa-
minations for the members of the Branch and as a
result, shall take necessary steps as to changing
then to lighter work, shortening their working hours
or granting them adequate recreation, etc.
LMPLOYIEHT AHD DISCHARGE:
Art. 11.
The Company shall employ or discharge employees
through negotiation with the Branch.
Art. 12.
The Company shall, through negotiation with the
Branch, discharge the person expelled from the
Branch.
Art. 13.
In case of promotion, appointment, transfer of
post, or revard and punishment of the members of
the Branch, the Company shall negotiate with the
Branch.
Art. 14.
In case of revision or amendment of any regula-
tions and systems and Company shall enact it upon
negotiation with the Branch.
Art. 15.
Regarding extra hour work, the start and finish of
work, recess, holidays, or leaves for greetings
and condolences which are beyond the description
of this Agreement, the Company shall negotiate
with the Branch before enactment.
PRODUCTION:
Art. 16.
In case of contracting work to sub-contractors or
ordering articles to be manufactured outside, the
Company shall negotiate with the Branch before doing
so.
Art. 17.
Regarding administrative matters of labor, mate-
rials, engineering, in the course of production,
the company shall negotiate with the Branch before
enactment.
WELFARE:
Art. 18.
The Company shall negotiate with the Branch before
enactment of matters on culture and education
promotion together with the management of welfare
racilities.
MANAGEMENT COUNCIL:
Art. 19.
The Company shall establish a Management Council
composed of members representing the Company and
the Branch, which will make every effort in the
democratization of management.

At the Council, the agenda of discussion shall

The Rules of Management Council shall be fixed
upon negotiations between the Company and the

Art. 20.
The matters which are deemed to be necessary to
confer upon by both the Company and the Branch and
matters regarding amendment of this agreement
shall be conferred between the Company and the
Branch.
UNION ACTIVITIES:
Art. 21.
The Company shall agree to have the Branch officers
devote themselves exclusively to union business.
Art. 22.
The Company shall agree to the Branch contacting
with or acting in concert with any friendly party
or other outer parties, or partaking in any other
activity.
Art. 23.
The Company shall agree to have the Branch, with
the understanding of the Company, to hold union
activities or education for the members of the
Branch, at a specific time during working hours.
Art. 24.
The Company shall offer management data necessary
to the  ranch.
Art. 25.
The Company shall allow union members freedom of
political advities--and of occupying any public
post, and shall not discriminate these members.
However as to their treatment the Company shall
negotiate with the Branch.
DISSOLUTION OF THE COMPANY:
Art. 26. The Company shall negotiate with the Branch on
measures that will greatly affect the members of
the Branch such as dissolution, suspension of
operation, amalgamation, separation, change of
ownership and transference or diversion of the
Company's assets.
Art. 27.
During a strike the Company shall not contract
labor service or any similar acts with others
concerned.
Art. 28.
The Company shall collect rent from employees who
board in the Company's housing and workers dormi-
tory; the rent fixed separately from their wages.
TERIM OF VALIDITY:
Art. 29. This Agreement is to remain in force for one (1)
year from Nov. 1, 1947.
However during the term of validity, the Agreement
can be revised by the mutual understanding of both
parties.

YOKOHAMA SHIPYARD & ENGINE WORKS
MITSUBISHI HEAVY INDUSTRIES, LTD.
3 Midoricho, Nishiku, Yokohama, Japan
REGULATIONS OF MANAGEIENT COUNCIL
YOKOHAMA SHIPYARD & ENGINE LORKS
MITSUBISHI HEAVY IIDUSTRIES, LTD.
Art. 1. In accordance with the provision of Article 19 of
the Labor Agreement which has been concluded
between the Yokohama Shipyard & Engine Works
Mitsubishi Heavy Industries, Ltd. (hereafter
referred to as "the Company") and the All Japan
Shipbuilding Workers' Union, Mitsubishi Yokohama
Shipyard Branch (hereafter referred to as "the
Branch") on November 1, 1947, a Hanagement Council
shall be established.
Art. 2.
This Council shall confer to decide on the follow-
ing matters:
1. Conditions of treatment such as wages, premium
rates, allowances, raises, the payment for over-
time, night and holiday work; time and means of
payment, working hours, time to start and
finish work, recess and its arrangement,
number of holidays and leaves, discharge
allowance, etc.
2.
Basic matters regarding discharge, employment,
time and qualification of raise and promotion,
transference of post and reward and punishment.
3.
Arrangement of labor, utilization of materials,
management of technique etc. in the process of
production.
4.
Basic matters regarding sub-contract and works
to be manufactured outside.
5.
The revision or abolition of any regulation
and work system.
6.
atters of culture and welfare such as indivi-
dual culture, education, public welfare,
health, fire prevention, etc.
7.
Matters concerning dissolution, suspension of
operation, amalgamation, separation, change of
oimership and transference or division of the
Company's assets, etc.
δ.
Amendment of this Agreement.
9.
Other matters which the Company and the Branch
deem necessary to confer with.
Art. 3.
The matters agreed on by the Council shall have
the same effect as the Labor Agreement.
Art. 4.
The Council shall be composed of eighteen (18)
members, with nine (9) members representing the
Company and nine (9) members representing the
Branch.
Art

ANDARD PORM NO. 64
TO
O - Mr. Claxton
DATE: July 13, 1948
FROM :
O - Mr. Ray
SUBJECT:
Comment on Proposed Policy Decision on Japanese Employment Abroad.
Selleian
(1) The reference to the Japanese Government is unnecessary during
the SCAP regime of control, - as SCAP can delegate any control function.
It may further be objectionable to some FEC countries as involving the
assumption of authority by Japanese Government as distinct from SCAP. This
reference is possibly undesirable as putting the Japanese Government as
such, prematurely in a position to criticize working or living standards in
other countries. The reference in para 5 to Japanese responsibility for
policing work contracts abroad is subject to the same comment. Protection
of Japanese interests abroad during the SCAP regime of control might better
be left nominally to SCAP.
of gualifications for proposed employment. Preferable phrase would be
"cleared", which indicates only the absence of objections for reasons such
as those of security.
(3) This is a "general principle" which would involve both difficulty
8
and expense in implementation. It would require extensive records and time-
consuming, complex and arbitrary evaluation of what are essentially variable
力
factors making up "normal living expenses in the country of employment." In
the case of Japanese seamen, the proposed language would require special inter-
pretation of both 'local currency" and "foreign exchange". On some voyages,
wages offered might not include any "foreign exchange useful for the purchase
of essential imports for Japan".
Existing foreign exchange controls over payments and remittances from
overseas to Japanese nationals should be adequate to protect the financial
interests of SCAP. The foreign employment of Japanese nationals in the present 
setting of overpopulation and a non-self-supporting economy is a clear benefit
to Occupation finance. Such remittances as Japanese employed abroad may
voluntarily make to the accounts of themselves, their dependents or other
Japanese in Japan would increase this benefit without new machinery or expense.
To establish new machinery and incur new expense in order to recapture the
"surplus" income of Japanese employed abroad over their "normal living expenses"
would tend to defeat the purpose of the policy both by discouraging applica-
tions and by generating expenses to offset the foreign exchange sought.
A parallel effort by OMGUS to control through JEIA the foreign exchange
"Surplus" earnings of German technicians temporarily employed abroad has
seriously hampered such employment.

=== Page 43 === (confidence 0.92)
-3-
yi
(4) The language here appears unnecessarily detailed and may be
too rigid to conform with established practices in different fields of
work.
(5) See comment on Japanese government functions in para. 1 above.
futh20.
(6) This paragraph contains two unrelated restrictions, one political,
and one economic. The economic restriction might better be consolidated
with a revised and generalized policy statement on economic controls to
replace present para.3.
Such of the above points as you may approve can be reflected in a
redraft policy paper that might be more palatable for introduction by a
member of FEC other than U. S.

DRAFT POLICY DECISION ACCEPTABLE
TO THE UNITED STATES GOVERNENT
ON THE EMPLOYMENT OF JAPANESE ABROAD
The Far Eastern Commission decides as a matter of polioy that
Japanese nationals shall be permitted to accept employment abroad under
regulations established by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
in Japan to accord with the following general principles:
(1) Employment shall be on a voluntary basis, with remuneration and
conditions for working and living acceptable at once to the Supreme
Commander, the Japanese Government, the Japanese employee and the foreign
cleared
employer.
(2) Applicants for employment must be individually approved by the
Supreme Commander's Headquarters.
(3) That proportion of total wages or salary sufficient for normal
living expenses in the country of employment shall be paid in local currency.
All acditional remuneration shall be paid to the account of the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers in Japan in foreign exchange useful for the
purchase of essentiel imporis for Japan. The Supreme Commander will cause
the Japanese Government to deposit an equivalent amount in yen at the military
conversion rate or other official rate that may subsequently be fixed to the
account of the employee or his dependents in Japan.
with punsion whare
shall bo
(4) The term of employment set forth in the contracts shell dot exceed
crucernad.
two years, but this period may be extended in case all parties to the agreement
all conteacbs stale pernit
desire. Hewever the employer or the employee shall be permitted to terminate
ans shace provid that
emplamaut
with specipid
the individual contract at any time by giving one monthls notiee, A In case the
astatispeinod of
employee avails himself of this privilege before the expiration of ene year's
specified
service, he shall be required to pay a portion of the costs of his
repatriation.

=== Page 45 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIAL
- 2 -
(5) The Japanese employee shall have unrestricted right of
communication with Japan, and it shall be the responsibility of the
Japanese Government to bring to the attention of the Supreme Commander
for appropriate action through the diplomatic channel any complaints
by Japanese employees regarding the fulfillment by foreign employers
of wcrk contracts. In order to ensure compliance with the terms of the
contract, arrangements should be made for inspection of working and
living conditions of the employees, should such be deemed necessary,
by duly authorized SCAf representatives or designees.
(6) Japanese employed abroad are not to engage in political activi-
ties of any sort during the period of their employment, and are not to
accuire property abroad except as may be required for normal living
purposes during the period of their employment.

Subject: Communist Influence in Japanese Labor
yR

894.00B
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Weshington.
Sir:
I have the honor to enclose a copy of a memorandum
of conversation dated July 27, 1948, surmarizing a con-
versation held between an officer of this Mission and
Mr. HIToTSUlATSU masaji, Democratic-Liberal rarty member
of the House of Councillors.
The opinions expressed by r. Hitotsumatsu in this
conversation are representetive of the present attitude
held by many of the more conservative Japanese political
and business leaders. His views are also believed to
reflect prevailing sentiment in the Democratic-Liberal
Party.
Kr. Eitotsunatsu reguested that his remarks be treated
confidentially because he wished tc peak freely and frankly
concerning aspects of the Cccupation which he felt were
detrimental to Japan's recovery. It has been noted that
3348
Japanese businessmen and political leaders are becoming
more inclined to discuss such matters frankly. This has
become particulerly true in regard to the labor problem,
since the better informed Japanese appear to be convinced
that the present unstable, and at times chaotic, labor
situation has a disastrous effect on Japanese economic
recovery and is a serious obstacle to attracting foreign
capital and enterprises to Japan. The surprisingly rapid
and favorable reaction of almost all Japanese to the
Supreme Commander's letter to Fremier ASHIDA of July 22,
1948 regarding revision of the National -ublic Service
Law by which the right of government workers to strike
will be prohibited is clear evidence of the extent to
which the Japanese public has become aroused over labor
excesses. (In this connection, reference is made to our
despatch no. 491 of August 3, 1948, on the subject "Japanese
reaction to Supreme Commander's letter concerning revision
S
of the Public Service Law".)
S
Mr. Hitotsumatsu stated that many Japanese with hom
he is associated are convinced that the present labor
pro.em
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 50 === (confidence 0.99)
fokyo's No. 490
ugust 3, 1948
CONFIDENTIAL
- 2 -
problem is in good part the result of the labor policies
pursued heretofore by the Occupation. Japanese labor has
acquired too many rights and priviliges wi thout sufficient
consideration given to the responsibilities of labor. He
believes that in any capitalistic economy there must be a
reasonable balance between labor and management, but that
under the Occupation labor has been favored at the expense
of management (and often at the expense of the general
public) with the result that the necessary equilibrium
X
between labor and management has been destroyed. The
Japanese communists, working through their powerful frao-
tions in the labor unions, have taken advantage of this
740.0
imbalance to exploit the labor movement's immaturity and
sudden acquisition of power for communist-inspired polit-
ical aims.
In Mr. Hitotsumatsu's opinion, SCAP labor policies,
especially in the earlier stages of the Occupation, have
therefore been partly responsible for the delay in econ-
omic recovery. Not only must the Japanese businessman
take into account abrupt price changes and irregular
supplies of materials, but he is also confronted by the
erratic and unpredictable behavior of labor as one of the
vital factors of production.
Japanese with whom Mr. Hitotsumatsu associates in
political and business circles are becoming apprehensive
that Japan's present rate of recovery will not prove
sufficient to ward off some form of depression which many
now feel is certain to come when the present period of
inflation ends. If such a depression occurs after the
Occupation is withdrawn, there is a growing fear that
public safety facilities will be inadequate to control the
communist-inspired unrest and disorders which may break
out. Mir. Hitotsumatsu believes that if such a situation
occurs after the Occupation is withdrawn, the communists,
with Soviet support, might readily seize power, and that
conservative elements, such as the Democratio-Liberal
Party, will surely be marked for liquidation under a com-
munist regime. For this reason, r. Hitotsumatsu is
convinced that many conservative Japanese are today
exceedingly hesitant to commit themselves openly, especially
in regard to any action involving opposition to the
communists.
In this connection, Mr. Hitotsumatsu noted that most
Japanese are perplexed by what appears to be an inconsis-
tency in United States policy. While opposing the spread
of communism in urope and elsewhere and while taking more
and more stringent measures against the communists in the
United States, the Occupation seems to give the Japanese
communists a free hand.
Mr. Hitotsumatsu also expressed the belief that
economic recovery is further handicapped by too much
detailed regulation of the Japanese economy by SCAP, and
by the lack of ability and understanding of many SCÁP
officials charged with handling economic problems.
The necessity
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 51 === (confidence 0.96)
okyo's No. 490
August 3, 1948
CONFIDENTIAL
- 3 -
The necessity for achieving a satisfactory readjust-
ment of labor-management relations from the standpoint of
assisting Japan's over-all economic recovery has been
stressed frequently in the Japanese press. In its issue
of July 29, 1948, Tokyo Shimbun noted particularly that
XR
foreign capital will remain hesitant to seek investment
in Japanese industry unless the Japanese labor movement
is placed upon a more rational and moderate basis. This
newspaper accordingly expressed the belief that greater
control.must be exercised over not only government workers,
as directed by the Supreme Commander's letter to the Prime
Minister, but over all labor union activity.
Respectfully yours,
W. J. Sebald
Acting Political Adviser

UNITED ST TES POLITICAL DVISER FOR JAPAN
EMORANDUR OF CONVERSATION
ugust 3, 1948
SUBJECT:
Communist Influence in Jopenese Labor
1RTICIFANTS: Lr. EITOTSUTSU Maseji, Lember of House
of Councillors
Lr. IITOTSUTSU Hiseshi, Secretary
r. Charles Nelson SPINKS, USPOLAD
On July 27, 1948 r. HITOT3UaTSU Maseji, Democratic-
Liberal Farty member of the louse of Councillors, with his
son and secretary, Mr. EITOTSU TSU Hisashi, called at this
i.ission to discuss the problem of communist influence in
the Japanese lebor movement with Foreign Service Officer
Charles Nelson SFINKS. The entire discussion was with the
elder Hitotsumatsu.
r. Hitotsumatsu is conservetive in his views but
should not be regarded es resctionary. He hes wide contacts
in politicel end business circles and reflects the more con-
servetive sentiments of J: panese business leaders. The
views he expressed in this meeting ere also believed to
reflect generelly the ettitude of the Democretic-Liberel
rarty.
i.r. Hitotsumatsu wes particularly emphatic in stressing
the dangers of coamunism in Japan as manifested in the labor
movement. he expressed the belief that the Occupation has
heretofore given too much emphasis to labor with the result
that lebor has been pushed too far ahead for its own good,
that in any capitalistic society there must be a reasonable
balance maintsined between labor and management, but such
equilibrium is inpossible to maintein if labor is unduly
favored by government at the expense of manegement.
r. Eitotsumatsu is convinced that Japanese labor has had too
many new advanteges and privileges thrust upon it with insuf-
ficient consideration given to the responsibilities of labor.
He feels that Japanese labor by both tradition and tempera-
ment is still unprepered for the new role it has assumed.
In Mr. Hitotsumatsu's opinion many of the new privil-
eges and adventages Japanese labor has acquired through
such laws as the Labor Standards Law and the Labor Relations
Adjustment Law ere ill-suited to present day conditions in
Japan. In the present stete of economic crisis, shorteges
and infletion, Japan cannot afford the luxury of such
requirements and conditions for labor. Mr. Hitotsumatsu
feels that Japan's economic recovery can only be brought
about in
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 53 === (confidence 0.99)
E cl. to Tokyo's no.490
A.gust 3, 1948
about in the final analysis by hard work on the part of
the entire population and that accordingly it is not
advisable to prescribe too rigidly the number of hours
a man should be &llowed to work, the minimum wage he
should receive and the exact conditions under which he
must work.
ir. Hitotsumatsu deplored the postwer tendency of
Japanese organized lebor to seek political objectives
through labor disputes and strikes. He pointed out that
this aspect of recurrent labor offensives has been consis-
tently fostered and promoted by the conmunist fractions
within labor uhions.
ir. Hitotsumatsu expressed the hope that the Occupa-
tion authorities in Japan would take a firmer stand toward
the communists. He seid many Japanese heve become exceed-
ingly puzzled by what appears to be a serious inconsistency
in United Stetes policy. while the United States has since
the end of the wer edopted a strong policy in opposing the
spreed of conmmunism in western Europe and other areas and
has become more and more severe in dealing with communists
and their sympathizers in the United States, the Japanese
communists appear to heve & virtually free hand.
He pointed out thet the situetion is beconing such
that only the Japanese comnunists appear able to criticize
the Occupation with impunity. Heretofore, the Occupation
hes teken only one major step which the Japanese believe
was an effective move against the communists, namely, the
Supreme Commander's banning of the proposed February 1
General Strike in 1947.
In view of widespread Japanese convictions that the
Occupation hes deliberately (and inconsistently) given the
communists a free hand, the Supreme Commender's letter to
Premier A3HIDA of July 22, 1948 came as a most welcome
surprise to virtually all Japanese except the communists.
In Mr. Hitotsumatsu's view, it is only the communists and
communist elements operating in the labor movement who will
be at a disadvantage when the National Fublic Service Law
is amended to prevent government workers from striking.
The communists have mede a particularly intense drive to
gain control of the government workers' unions, for, as
Mr. Hitotsumatsu pointed out, labor offensives conducted
by these unions are most likely to attain direct political
results, such as the overthrow of a cabinet, which is one
of the primary principles of conmunist strategy.
Mr. Hitotsumetsu wes asked if he would care to name
any specific examples of activity by which the Japanese
have been led to believe that the Occupetion has been
partial to the communists. Mr. Hitotsumatsu hesitated to
name specific points for in doing so he could not help but
criticize the Occupation, but to speak in strict confidence
and with complete frenkness he said he felt the principal
trouble was to be found in the initial conduct of SCAP
labor policies by the Labor Division of the Economic and
Scientific Section. r. Hitotsumatsu desired to emphasize,
however, that a significant change for the better has taken
place in the Labor Division since Mr. J. S. KILLEN assumed
charge, but that it was now difficult to correct many of
the abuses
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 54 === (confidence 0.99)
301. to.
okyo's no.490
- 3 -
ugust 3,1948
the abuses which had been inspired by his predecessor's
policies. Mr. Hitotsumatsu said that in the opinion of
many the policies pursued by the Labor Division in the
early stages of the Occupation under Mr. Theodore COHEN
(presently an economic adviser to the Chief of ESS) had
not only done much harm to Japan's economic recovery but
had in the long run proved a disservice to the Japanese
labor movement. Many Japanese are convinced that Mr.Cohen
deliberately favored labor at the expense of management
and that he was largely responsible for creating a most
undesirable imbalance between labor and management by
attempting to thrust labor too far ahead for its own good
and by his generally hostile attitude toward employers in
labor disputes. As a result, labor not only received
inflated ideas of its own rights and prerogatives, but
employers were actually intimidated to the point where
they lost initiative and enthusiasm. Thus, in
Mr. Hitotsumatsu's opinion, management has become more
and more uneasy about embarking on plans for expansion or
rehabilitation for feer that labor will always be an unpre-
dictable factor in production calculations, while at the
same time frequent strikes, slow downs and other labor
disputes, inspired to a good extent by SCAP labor policy,
have further retarded production.
Notwithstanding recent gains in the Japanese economy,
Lir. Hitotsumatsu is convinced that Japan's recovery to date
will be wholly inadequate to werd off some form of depres-
sion when the present period of inflation comes to an end.
This depression will result in large-scale unemploy-
ment and wili thereby provide the Japanese communists with
a most favorable opportunity. Many Japanese are afraid
that this period of depression may come about after the
Occupation is withdrawn. Mr. Hitotsumatsu does not feel
that the present public safety facilities will be adequate
to cope with communist-inspired unrest and disorders which
may take place in such a period of depression. Conserva-
tive Japanese are also afraid that Soviet aussia may use
this opportunity to intervene in Japanese affairs and
thereby succeed in putting the communists in power. Remote
as these possibilities may seem at the present moment,
Mr. Hitotsumatsu is convinced that the fear of such
developments haunts many Japanese and thereby makes them
reluctant to take a cleer-cut stand on political issues
which involve any opposition to the communists.
Mr. Hitotsumatsu stressed that members of the Democraic-
Liberal Party, which has been frequently branded within
and without J&pan as a "reactionary" element, feel they
cannot be indifferent to the possibility that they will be
earmarked for liquidation if the communists ever'come to
power. The possibility of & severe depression, which might
bring the communists to power with outside support, is
therefore all the more elarming.
Mr. Hitotsumatsu is also convinced that economic
recovery in Japan is being seriously handicapped by too
much detailed regulation of the economy by SCAP. Economic
controls have become so ramified and rigid that there is
little possibility of rapid and flexible adjustments to
meet the ever-changing situation. Working through the
cumbersome bureauoracy of the Japanese government and the
complicated structure of the Occupation, it takes so long
to effect
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 55 === (confidence 0.99)
incl. to Tokyo's no.490
August 3, 1948
- 4 -
to effect changes in control regulations that by the time
such changes are finally debated, drafted and put in force,
an entirely new situation hes developed for which the
changes are no longer appliceble. At the same time,
Mr. Hitotsumatsu felt he should point out that Japanese
business men have little faith in the ability or under-
stending of many SCAP officials in the Economic and
Scientific Section with whom they are compelled to deal.
Some of these individuals have had no previous experience
in the particular line of business with which they are
concerned, while others are totally unfamiliar with or
indifferent to the problems which are peculiar to production
in postwar Japan.

U - Mr. Ibvett:
THROUGH: S/S
Subject: Draft Telegram to SCAP regarding Japanese Cabinet
Order of July 31, 1948 on Rights of Japanese
Government Employees.
Discussion:
The Cabinet Order in question, issued after receipt of a letter
from General MacArthur, is considered by the Departments of State and
Labor to violate U. S. and FEC labor policy for Japan, and to have
other undesirable political consequences in Japan, the U. S. and else-
where.
This draft message constitutes an interpretation of previously
established policy as applied to the objectionable provisions of the
Cabinet Order, and a request for early information from SCAP as to his
plans for rectifying the cited violations.
In view of the urgent interest of the Acting Secretary of Labor,
and since this topic was discussed at the Staff Meeting of August 10,
8-1048
the text of the draft message is submitted for your personal clearance.
Recommendation:
That you approve the attached draft message for transmittal to
the Department of the Army for clearance and dispatch through Army
channels to SCAP as a joint message from Labor, State and Army.
Concurrences:
ITP (Martin)
FE (Butterworth)
O (Saltzman)
Chiland A. Cextg
Dept. of Labor (Acting Secretary)

Subject: Draft Telegram to SCap regarding Japanese Cabines

Discussion:
The Cabinet Order in question, issued after receipt of a letter
from General MacArthur, is considered by the Departments of State and
Labor to violate U. S. and FEC labor policy for Japan, and to have
other undesirable political consequences in Japan, the U. 3. and else-
where.
This draft message constitutes an interpretation of previously
established policy as applied to the objectionable provisions of the
Cabinet Order, and a request for early information frum SCAP as to his
plans for rectifying the cited violations.
In view of the urgent intarest of the Acting Secretary of Labor,
and since this topic was discussed at the Staff Mesting of August 10,
the text of the draft message is submitted for your personal clearance.

the Department of the Army for clearance and dispatch through Army
channels to SCAP as a joint message from Labor, State and Army.

suajscr: Collective Bargaining and Strike Rights of Japanose
Government Employees.
Summary
The Japanese Cabinet, following the receipt of
letter from SCaP, has issued an ordinance denying collectivo
bargaining and strike rights to all employees of the Japanoso
Government. We believe this ordinance is too sweeping in
its provisions in that it restrains large numbers of Japan
workers from exercising rights to which they should be
entitled, and therefore that the Department should interot
itself in the matter with the intent of correcting the
situation.
State-War-Navy Coordinating Committee (Swncc) Papo
92/1, dated 16 November 1945, Treatment of Japanese Workere
Organizations, and declared by the Secretaries of Stato,
War and Navy to be United States policy on 28 December 1945
stated:
"As soon as Workers' organizations are
formed.......they should be encouraged to bargain
collectively with employers regarding terms of
employment, subject to such policies on wages
laws and working conditions as are established
by the Japanese Government."
Far Eastern Commission policy decision, dated
6 December 1946, entitled: Principles for Japanese Trade
Unions, stated:
"Trade unions should be encouraged to
negotiate with the employers on behalf of their
members regarding terms and conditions of
employment."
Strikes, according to both of the above polioy stato-
ments, were to be prohibited only when the occupation
authorities considered that work stoppages would direotly
prejudice the objectives and needs of the occupation.

Japanese law and the Japanese Constrtution also

workers to organise and to bargain and act colleotively is

Discussions have been going on for some months between
the Labor Division of the Economio and Scientifio Section.
ScAP, and the Government Seotion relative to possiblo amond
ment of the provisions of the new National Publio Servioo
Law and the bearing of such amendment upon the colleotive
bargaining rights of Japanese Government employees. These
employees, numbering approximately 2,700,000, include not
only workers with administrative funotions, such as are
covered by the term "civil service employees" in the American
sense, but also all employees of government-owned industries
such as the railway, telephone, telegraph and postal systems
and of the salt, tobacco and camphor monopolies. A large
majority of the total are in the latter category. Japanese
law has not permitted the administrative employees to
strike, though they have been granted the right to conduot
bargaining negotiations with government agenoies. The
larger group, operating employees as they are called, have
had both bargaining and strike rights.
On July 22 SCAP addressed a letter to Prime Minister
Ashida recommending amendments to the National Publio Sorvice
Law. This message, though somewhat ambiguous on a number of
points, recommended in effect that colleotive bargaining
rights be taken away from all exoept those who were employed
by the government monopolies and the railways. The latter
group, roughly 20% of the total, were to be allowed mediation
and arbitration privileges. Ali of the 2,700,000, it was
recommended, be denied the right to strike.
The Japanese Cabinet immediately announced it regarded
the letter as a directive to be put into force at once by
Cabinet ordinance. Such an ordinance, based on an Imperial
Ordinance (September 20, 1945) authorizing the Cabinet to
issue any orders necessary to implement SCAP directives under
the Potsdam Declaration, would take legal precedence over
existing Japanese laws. This ordinance, finally issued on
July 3l and effeotive from that date, went even farther than
was recommended in SCAP's letter by denying colleotive
bargaining and striking rights to all employees "of the
National Government or local publio entities." Fines and
a jail sentence of up to one year were decreed for violation
of this ordinance. All existing labor contracts affecting

It is our belief that the changes in the previously

by this Cabinet ordinance go further than is warranted or
required by the situation in Japan. We agree that govern-
ment workers who may legitimately be called Civil Sorvioo
employees should be covered by Civil Service regulations and
denied rights of full collective bargaining and, of courso,
of striking. The bulk of the workers affeoted by this now
regulation, however, are not of this type. A defense can be
made, in the case of civil service workers, against a chargo
that employees are being denied rights laid down by the poliey
decisions named above as well as by the Japanese Constitution
No such defense, particularly in regard to the denial of
collective bargaining rights, can be made in the case of
non-civil-service employees of government. The almost uni-
versal practice followed in democratio countries for employeos

practice not be tolerated in case a nationally vital servioe

however, even employees of the government tobacoo, salt and
camphor monopolies are denied both strike and collective
bargaining rights.
This is a matter of legitimate interest to the State
Department because: (1) The changes described above are so
great as to raise questions as to whether they do not in
effect constitute a change of polioy and are inconsistent
with SWNCC and FEC directives. (2) FEC member countries
especially those with labor governments, such as the United
Kingdom, Australia and New Zealand, are almost certain to
raise objections regarding this denial of labor rights both
within the FEC and to the Department directly. (3) Leadors

""The Cabinet hereby established the Cabinet order conoernin
the temporary measures to be taken in consequence of the let
of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers to the Premier
dated July 22, 1948 under the Imperial Ordinance No. 548 of
1945 concerning the orders to be issued in pursuanoe of the
acceptance of the Potsdam Declaration.
"'1. Those persons who hold positions as employees of the
National Government or local publio entities, regardless of
whether they are appointed or employed (hereinafter referred
to as publio employees) shall not have the right, to be
exercised against the National Government or local publio
entities, of collective bargaining as usually understood with
its coercive character supported by the strike threat. Publio
employees or their organizations, however, may not be denied
the freedom, within the restriction of the present Cabinet
ordinance, to negotiate with the appropriate agency of the
National Goyernment or local publio entity in the sense of
being able freely to present individually or colleotively.
through their representatives, their complaints, opinions
desires, and grievances and to support the same by adequate
opportunity for discussion and the submission of evidence
"All acts heretofore taken by the Government in personnel
matters affecting public servants will be valid, so long as
they do not violate the spirit of the limitations imposed by
this Cabinet order, or were not taken in contravention of such
limitations.
"'All mediation proceedings now pending in which the National
Government or local public entities are parties will be
suspended. The National Personnel Authority will hereafter
be the agency charged with the protection of the interests of
public servants.
":2. No public employees may resort to strike or engage in
delaying or other dispute tectics which tend to impair the
efficienoy of operation of National Government or local publio
entities.
"Those who act in violation of the provision of the preceding
paragraph regardless of their status of being publio employees
may not assert their rights derived from such appointment or
employment against the National Government or local publio ontitiom

=== Page 71 === (confidence 0.99)
DIVISION OF
NORTHEAST ASIAN AFFAI
Japanese Government Ordinance of 3lst
July regarding Rights of Public Employees.
AUG 149
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
The Japanese Government issued this ordinance after
Mr. Ashida received General McArthur's letter of 22nd July,
which we do not believe he intended to be a directive. We
are concerned that the ordinance puts industrial workers
on the railways and in the salt, camphor and tobacco monopolies
on the same footing as civil servants in the government as
regards the right to strike and bargain collectively. We
consider that in this respect the Japanese Government's
ordinance goes too far, and in fact we notice that General
McArthur's suggestion that employees in these categories might
well be excepted from the regular civil service, with which
suggestion we emphatically agree, appears to have teen ignored
by the Japanese Government. In the view of His Majesty's
Government it would be wrong that industrial workers in these
categories should be deprived of the right to strike and bargain
collectively which is normally enjoyed by industrial workers in
general. This could be remedied if General McArthur would
insist upon compliance by the Japanese Government with that part
of his letter which deals with this aspect of the situation.
Unless this is done soon the criticism is bound to be made that
the action of the Japanese Government conflicts with the spirit
of FEC-O45/5 and should not have been allowed.
2.
As regards civil servants in the more strict
definition of the term, His Majesty's Government consider that
it is unfortunate that they should now be placed in a position
markedly inferior to that which they enjoy in many other democratic
countries. We should not wish to object to their being deprived
of the right to strike; but the right of the civil servant to
organise and bargain on a footing of equality coupled with the right
of appeal to some form of arbitration tribunal has existed for
many years in the United Kingdom and the system functions smoothly.
There is no "coercive character" in such a system, nor is there any
suggestion of a "strike threat". The mere right of petition to an
organ of the Japanese Government, which is all that is at present
left open to the Japanes. civil servant, is clearly a poor
substitute.

Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: . September 4, 1948
SUBJECT:
Denial of Collective Bargaining and Strike Rights of
Japanese Goverrment and Publio Employees.
PARTICIPAWTB: Nr. Jamss S. K1llen, recently chief of SCAP's Iabor
Division, Economic and Soientific Section.
Mr. Philip B. Sullivan, IIH

COPIES TO:

(Noto: Mr. Killon resigned as chiof of the Labor Division of SCAP Hoadquerters
as a result of General MacArthur's recommendations of July 22 concerning the
labor rights of Japanese public eaployees. He is resuming duties as a Vice-
President of the International Brotherhood of Pulp, Sulphite, and Paper Mill
Workers, AFL.)
SUMMARY
1. General MacArthur's decision in the isaue of labor righte ve. civil
service status for Japanese public employees was made largely on the basie of
the strike record and strike potential of the umions conoerned, although the
Labor Division was not arguing for retention of the right to strike. The
General rejected the case for collective bargaining unaupported by right or
threat of strike.
2. In delivering to Premier Ashida the SCAP lotter of July 22, General
Whitney, chief of the Goverment Section, also gave the Premier a blueprint
of the course of action subsequently followed by the Japanese Government in
implementing it.
3. Mr. Sobald, US Political Adviser, privately expressed to Mr. Killen
his conoern over the international repercuesions he expected, and his discomfiture
over having to dofend the letter and its implemsntation without having been
consulted at loast as to its internaticnal aspects.

=== Page 75 === (confidence 0.98)
11 -
RESTRICTED
4. Mr. Killen resigned because, as an American trede-unionist,
he felt that he could not reconcile himeelf to administering a policy
with which he was in virtually ccmplete disegreement; and that under the
ciroumstanoes he himself could do less than the Japanese and the Allied
Powers concerned to obtain a modification of the policy. Although a
statement of his position was not carried by the Japanese press, its
contents beoam ganerally known.
5. Protests against the Japanese Government's implementation of
the SCAP letter, and by implication against the letter iteelf, came
from a muoh wider area of the labor movement than the unions directly
concerned, largely because of a fear that the rights of labor ganerally
would be subject to attack.
6. Nr. Killen believes that the basic Allied objective with respect
to labor in Japan is the development of a strong, sound, and autonomous
labor movement. The movement is in need of corrections, some of which
can best be supplied by guidance, some by administrative means, and only
a few by logislation. In his view, however, the polioy presently in
force is little short of suppression; it will probably not assist the
emergence of a militant and intelligent trade-union leadership that can
outemart the Communists on their oum ground. The present situation, he
fears, may react to the advantage of the Commmist Party in that it
constitutes the denial by US authority of basic rights deemed imperative
in building free institutions in Japan, thereby discrediting our professed
policy to "demooratize" that nation.

MR. KILIEN'S REMARKS ON THE DENIAL OF COLLECTIVE BARGADNING
AND STRIKE RIGHTS OF JAPANESE COVERNIENT AND PUBLIC DPLOYEES
1. General MacArthur's Docision
Several days before General NacArthur's letter of July 22, a seven-
hour debate on the iasue of the labor rights of government employees was
held in the General's presence. The Goverment Section, arguing for the
inclusion within the regular civil service of all goverment mmployees
and the enmulment of their labor rights granted under laws then in force,
was represented by General Whitney and Nr. Kades, its chief and deputy
chief, and by Mr. Blaine Hoover and Mr. MacCoy, civil service experts.
General Marquat and tr. Killen represented the Economic and
Soiontific Section, arguing for:
(al The retention of govermment employees' right to organize and
to bargain colloctively.
(b) The use of medistion and compulsory arbitration as a substitute
for ths custorary weapone of stribes and lockouts.
(c) The exerciss by the Diet of over-riding authority where
necesaary and/or appropriate.
Although virtually the entire top level of SCAP Headquarters was
present, discussion was carried on almost exolusively by General MacArthur,
Mtr. Hoover, and Mr. Killen.
The debate was divided into an afternoon and an evoning seusion. During
the afternoon, kr. Killen believed that he was at least holding his owm.
For exemple, General MacArthur poveral times declared himself in agreement
with the principle that teachers and employees of other non-essential
goverment aotivities should retain the right to bargain collectively in
the usually accepted sense. Br. Hoover, confrouted by quastions such as
"Should coal miners be recruited by compotitive civil service exsminations"?
did not make a direot reply but confined himself to arguing that the
authority of the state mst be suprame. Even in the face of the admittedly
successful Whitley Coumcils in the British oivil service, Mr. Hoover folt
British experience had no bearing on the Japanese situation. He disagreed
that labor rolations in the Tennessee Valley Authority were good and
maintained that the US made a grievous mistake in exoluding TvA from civil
service coverags.
Upon entoring SCAP's office for the evening session, the conferees
found General Whitney already there. It became evident during the
subeequent disoussions that General MacArthur had during the recese been
largely persuaded to the viewpoint of the Government Soction. For example,
he reversed hie earlier vieus in favor of full labor rights for employees
of non-essential goverment ssrvices; he repeatedly called attention to
the past and then-threatening strikes of government employees; and he cited
with approval the principle that the arthority of the state must be supreme,
above challenge, and cortainly not on equal footing in any respeot with

that of a minority group. In particular, General MacArthur expounded

was a "powerless state"; therefore the possibility of a mass uprising
