2. The Labor Division's Colloctive Bargaining Proposal
Throughout the debate, Mr. Killen sought to clarify the conoept of
collective bargaining unsupported by threat of striks. This conoept is
embodied in the civil service legielation of the United Kingicm,
Australia, New Zealand and other democracies. It is practiced in some
government enterprises in the United States, such as the TVA, the
inland waterways, and the Alaskan railroads, as well as state and
municipal enterprises. It places employer and employee on equal footing
for purposes of negotiating a solution of differenoes; it imposes on each
a responsibility to negotiato in good faith for a solution; and in the
ovent of failure to reach a solution autonomously it provides for
tripartite mediation machinery or for binding arbitration in those cases
deemed proper matters for arbitration.
According to Mr. Killen, this concept is a relatively new one in labor
relations within govermment service but has proved its value especially in
the United Kingiom. It has developed in response to the need for adepting
traditional labor rights to modern conditions in whioh work stoppages in
activities of direct public interest would constitute a serious and
immediate threat to the publio welfare. This concept is embodied in
Japan's Labor Relations Adjuetment Lew, which denies to the government's
administrative (as opposed to manual or operating) employees the right
to strike.
The treditional concept of colleotive bargaining of a coeroive nature
supported by threat of strike dominated the thinking of the Goverment
Sootion representatives. In Mr. Killen's view, these men either could not
grasp the distinction between the two concepts, or, more likely, would not,
inasmmch as it was to their advantage to define collective bargaining in
terms that implied not only a theoretical derogation of the authority of
the state but also the possibility of a real and dangerous challenge.

3. Interpretation of the SCAP Letter
In Mr. Killen's opinion, the SCAP letter was clearly intended to be
a directive in all but the nams. He conveyed this opinion to the
Japanese who immediately came to his office -- KAto Kanju, Minister of
Labor; DOBASHI Kazuyoshi, chairman of the commmication workers' union;

and others. Moreover, Mr. Killen learned on what he consideres

letter to Premier Ashida, also delivered a blueprint of the course of
action later followed by the Japanese Govermment, including an outline
of the Cabinet Ordinance of July 31.
Mr. Killen does not find the SCAP letter convincing. Inter alia,
he considers that the lotter failed to meet squarely and deal with the
collective bargaining iseue, and that it over-emphasized the question of
the right to strik -- a point which Mr. Killon says had long ago been

to include at least the telephone and telegraph workers and the employess
of other enterprise-type activities (national and local) in the same

4. Reactions Within Headquartors
Prior to hie departure, Mr. Killen had a conversation with Mr. Sebald,
who privately expressed his great concern over the international
reperoussions that he anticipated in the first instance in the Allied
Council. Aocording to Mr. Killen, Mr. Sobald appeared to feel that the
Department of State and its Mission in Tokyo had been put in the position
of having to defend the SCAP lotter and its implemontation without having
been consulted at least as to its international aspects.
Mr. Killen says that some groupe within Headquarters, notably the
Industrial Division of Economic end Scientific Section, immodiately sought
to have the application of General MacArthur's polioies as stated in the
letter extended to employees of vital private enterprises such as coal
mining and eleotric pover transmission.
5. Mr. Xillen's Resignation

labor people hounded him for a statmment of his position, tr. Killen
detormined to resign. As an American trade-unionist, ho felt that he

7. Beeds of the Japenese Labor Movement
Mr. Killen believes that the basic Allied objective with respeot to
Labor in Japan is the dovelopment of a strong, sound, and autonomous
labor movement. Without such a movement as a basis, he feels, there is
little point in legislative protection such as that afforded by the Labor
Standards Law. of the three major reform programs -- labor, land, and zaibateu
zaibateu -- the labor program is perhape the most diffioult to implement.
The Labor progrem involves the development of an organized, responeible and
free morement of Japanese workers exercising their rights and bearing
their responsibilities as members of a demooratic society. The developmont
of such a movement can be approached only partially by legislation; more
must be done by education and guidance. Once in form and motion, the
labor movement can protest iteelf.
Duriag the first two years of the occupation, the labor movement was
spoon-fed. Now it is in need of corrections. From Mr. Killen's basio
viewpoint, some of the needed corrections can best be supplied by guidance
and pressure; acme by administrative means; and only a few by legislation.
During the last few monthe of his temure, Mr. Killen sought to employ
the first two approaches. For example, be urged the Cabinet to undertaks
the step-by-step reduction and eventual elimination from govermment
payrolls of the 5,0o0-odd full-time union officials. Bo also urged that
the govermment insist on the inclusion in future labor agreemente of
disputes adjustment machinery; of provisions for the non-payment of
workors for time spent on stris; and of definite tsrmination clauses.
These proposals wore in procoss of implementation as of July.
As for legisletion, Mr. Killen favors for Japan the enactment of levs
providing for the fair and open olection of union officials; for the
publication of umion finances; and perhaps also for the holding of
rogular union meetings on the local level. Suoh logislation was not
pressed during the second national Diet session because of Headquarters
policy. The Socialist Party, a member or the coalition government, had
committed itself to opposing revision of the lebor laws, and it was
generally agreed within Hesdquarters to bep the coalition govermment going
at least through the period of greatest economic crisis.
In Mr. Killen's eyes, the policy in force at present is little short
of suppression of the heart of the Japanese labor movement, and, unless
modified, it is likely to have suppressive effects on the rest of the
labor movement.
Some observers, according to Xr. Xillen, advence the view that the
underlying fundementals of free trade unionism have now been fully
explained and dooumented for the benefit of Japanese worksrs and that they
and their umions must now sink or svim depending on their ccceptence of
the advioe and assistance already given. Mr. Killen considers that this
view has a bermel of truth. However, he feels that the risks inherent in
making such a view the basis of policy would be great indeed, partioularly
if the same sort of policy wore not simultaneously adopted with respect to
Japanese sooiety as a whole.

.6 -
The basic correction needed is cno that must come in the long rum
from the Japanese themselves, Mr. Killen believes. This is the emergence
of a militant, intelligent and "scrappy" umion leadership that can
outsmert the Comemmists on their own ground. There is a small mumber of
such leaders at present. The Socialist trade-union Leaders have the
virtue of being anti-Commnmist and of keeping a large segmont of the
movement out of the Commmmist fold. However, with few exceptione, they
are not true trade-union leaders: they are more interested in the
Booialist Party and in their own political fortunes then in the welfare
of the membership. Their umions are only slightly more demooratic in
organization end practice than are the Commmmist-led unions. The Comumists
provide the militancy and the acumen that are needed, and undeniably do
bring benefits to the rank and file, but they prostitute their unions to
the ends of the Commmist Party and the Soviet Union.
Perhaps the chief hope for the emergenoe of the leadership needed
lies in the growing awareness among the Japanese of the nature of the
Commmamist Party. Mr. Killen fears, however, that the present situation
may react to the advantage of the Comnmist Party in that it constitutes
the denial by B authority of basic rights deemed imperative in building
free institutions in Japan, thereby disorediting our professed polioy to
"democratize" the nation.

Note: 3everal English terms heve commonly been translated into Japanese expressions
which confuse rather than explain the meaning. A fuller explanation, therefore, is
necessary of the most important of these terms.
Local union Ln America this is a body, usually composed of workers in a single fac-
tory or office, whose members formulate policy in frequent meetings. Japanese union
structure is often more complicated. The members of various "tani kumiai" may meet
frequently at the factory and elect delegates to a "shibu kumiai", composed of work-
ers in saveral factories, which is the pclicy-making body but which holds a full
membership meeting only once a year. Thus, monthly'meetings and policy-making,
which are both carried on in an American local union, may be separated in Japan. So
the term "local union" is sometimes translated as "tani kumiai" and sometimes as
"shibu kumiai". A third torm, "chiho kumiai", which is also used. may refer to the
"shibu kumiai," or to a still broader district oreanization.
Management council The "keiei kyogi kai" is an institution peculiar to Japan, where-
in thé company and the union try to settle all kinds of labor problems, big and
small, and also consider production problems. It has some points in common with the
"factory committees" found in many European countries. In America, particularly du-
ring the War, "Labor-Managoment Committees" were cet up in some factories, but these
were advisory bodies on production mattcrs only. There are similar Joint Product-
ion Committees" in England. In America big lubor problems are handled instoad by'
collective bargaining between the union's "negotiating comhittee" and the company.
and small problems by the "griovance committce." In'short, there are vurious bodies
which might be translated the same way into Jupanese, but shich have different funo-
tions.
Collective bargcining' This is a mo thod of settling differoncos betweon labor and
managoment peacefully, by negotinting compromises. It applios mainly to the discus-
sions preceding the signing of a contract, but the negotiation of grievances is also
considerod by somo as collective bargaining. Th bargaining is conducted by a small
committee on behalf of all the mombers. However, many Japanese have misundorstood
tho turm "collcctive" to meen thut everyone takes part in the negotiations. The idea
of "bargaining" is not well undorstood cither. Japaneso unionists often say: "We
try to sottlo problems in the manugement council, but if that fails, wo go into col-
lective bargaining." what thoy mean is that thoy then cenne bargaining, and begin
dispute tactics.
Gricvance machinery This'is usod to sottlc simll issues, involving a single worker
or small group of workers, urising aftor the contract is signed. It should not bo
used to settle wages, the closed shop, or other big issues affecting all the workers;
these shoulu be sottlod beforo the contract is signed, by collective bargaining, a
strike, or the Labor Rolations Committec. The standard but misleading tranalation
of grievance machinery, "funso shori kikan", lhas holped give Japanose employers and
unions the'falso impression that it is intended to provide compulsory arbitration of
all issues, big and small. A more accurate translation would be "kujo shori kikan"
or "igi noshitato totsuzuki". but these torms are not familiar to newspapor readers.
Conciliation, modiation, and arbitration Those throe words have distinct meanings.
The conciliator trics to bring tho two disputing partios togethor without proposing
any terms of sottlement. Tho modiator proposes torms, but they are not binding.
The arbitrator's proposals are binding. In Japan these throe tochniques have not
been differentiated, and so there were no corresponding words in Japáneso. Three
words, "Assen", "chotei" and "chusai" have been arbitrarily selected, but they are
not self-explantory to the Japanese and so are often confusod by interpreters.
Closed shop The Japanese have adoptod the English 'oxpression. but they apply it to
the union shop. Undor the true closed shop systom. the employer can Kire only
workors who already belong to the union. Under the union shop system, the omployer
can hiro anyono he wants. but the net omployees must lator join the union. Most
Japanese contracts are called closed shop contracts, but they are roally union shop
contracts.

CONFIDENTIAL
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum ofConversation
TELEPHONE
DATE: September 20, 1948
SUBJECT:
Japanese Employment Abroad
894.504/9-2048
PARTiciPANTS: r. Tswen-ling Tsui, Counsellor, Chinese Embassy
O - r. J. rranklin Ray, Jr.
12:15 P. . and 5:15 F. . on September 17, 1948
COPIES TO:
NA
A- hir. Allison
0 - i.r. Ray
FOS/J - r. Schuler
O - Files
0
- i.r. Claxton
FEC - Lr. Blakeslee
ED
- L.r. Whitman
1-1408
ir. Tsui called with reference to a cable received by his Embassy on
September 14, 1943 from Nanking. This cable stated that the Chinese Mssion
at Tokyo hau submitted, on instructions from Nanking, a request to SCAP for
approval and assistance in arranging for the employment of 20 Japanese tech-
nicians by the kwangtang Frovincial government at Couton, to re-install a
paper mill owned by the Provincial government which had been looted by the
Japanese and later restituted to China.
This cable further reported, accoroing to r. Tsui, that SCAP had in-
CS/A
dicated no objection to the proposed employment, but had asked for Washington
epprovel. The Embassy hed therefore been instructed to request the Department
to give its approval to SCAP for the employment of these Japanese nationals
in China.
(The SCeP recuest for Washington approval vihich was referred to in the
Chinese cable can be identified as Ci I. 1572 of September 9, which was
answered by the Army, after informel consultation with the Department, in
C. OUT 89065 of September 11, 1948. This outgoing message advised SCAP that
the Chinese request in question "is matter for your administrative discretion",
and informed SCAF that the subject had previously been ciscussed with our
Embassy at Nanking).
The writer promised to look into the matter and aavise lir. Tsui of his
findings.
w88
SEP 27 1948
Le
After
FILED
RONFIDENDAL
3029

=== Page 108 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
After consultetion with NA (ir. Allison), the writer called Mr. Tsui
back to inform him that the Depertment had no objection to the granting of
the Chinese request by SCAP, on detailed terms acceptable to SCAP, and that
SCAP had been so informed. The Depertment was surprised, however, at the
apparent discrepancy between the Chinese position with reference to this re-
quest and the general position heretofore taken by the Chinese delegation to
the Far Eastern Commission in discussions of policy for travel of Japanese
nationals for legitimate commercial or cultural purposes. In such discussions,
the Chinese delegation had shown a disposition to restrict and limit the
approval of such travel more than was considered necessary or desirable by
the U. S. delegation.
The writer asked Mr. Tsui whether the current request for American co-
operetion in arranging for this travel end employment signified a change in
the previous attitude of the Chinese government towards the travel of Japanose.
Such a change, it was indicated, would be welcomed by the Department. Mr. Tsui
replied that he was not personally prepared to comment, but that he would re-
port this enquiry to Dr. Tan and the Ambassador. He stated that his instruc-
tions from Nanking did not indicate whether theimmediate request, originating
with officials interested in the particular employment sought, had been
coordinated with those officers concerned with the formulation of Chinese
government policy on matters before the FEC. r. Tsui stated that he personally
saw the connection and would seek a review of the matter.
hir. Tsui was then told that we would be reluctant to comment openly in
meetings of the Commission on the apparent discrepancy in Chinese attitudes
to whichhis attention had been called, and trusted that this would not be
made necessary by positions taken in the future by the Chinese delegation.
The writer expressec the hope that upon due consideration, the Chinese dele-
getion to the FEC would find it possible to join the U. S. delegation in
supporting policies which would permit the travel abroad of Japanese for
worthy and legitimate purposes without nnnecessary and burdeneome restrictions,
and asked that the Department be informed in due course of the results of
Lr. Tsui's discussions with Dr. Tan and the Ambassador.

=== Page 111 === (confidence 0.97)
SECRET
ces.
TO
Mr. Saltzman
DATE: October 5, 1948
FROM:
J. F. Ray, Jr.
SUBJECT:
Applicability of FEC O45/5 to Japanese Government Workers
In reply to Mr. Draper's question, the Department regards the FEO policy
decision "Principles for Japanese Trade Unions" (FEC-O45/5) as generally
applicable to organizations of government employees, subject to such qualifica-
tions and limitations as arise from the respective responsibilities and duties
of various categories of government employees. Our views as to these limita-
tions are as follows:
1. The right to strike, provided in para 5, may be curtailed or denied
to any or all government employees as such.
2. The right to mediation and arbitration procedure to resolve disputes
not settled by direct negotiation, provided in para 4, may be denied to per-
sonnel of the ordinary civil service including the postal service, by reason
of their duties which are peculiar to government, although not to industrial
employees of the government, such as railway, communications, monopoly, con-
68
struction, maintenance and custodial workers, whose functions are indistin-
guishable from those or corresponding workers in private employment.
3. The right to form or join employee organizations at all as provided
55
in paras. 1 and 2, may be denied to personnel of the police, fire, coast guard.
penal institutions services, or any others similarly assigned, by reason of
f
their responsibility to protect public safety and order.
In deference to the possibly conflicting views expressed heretofore by
SCAP and the directly contrary statement made by Mr. Sebald to the Allied
Council, this position is stated negatively rather than positively in the
attached draft message, and the Department wishes to avoid public statement
of its position. Such public statement would be embarrassing to SCap and
USPOLAD and woulå generate prolonged and undesirable controversy of a legal-
istic character over details with friendly FEC countries whose legislation
and practice differs from that of the United States.
The Department believes that the application in Japanese law and practice
of the principles set forth above will obviate attack from all interested
countries except the UsSR and enlist general support for the action taken
without requiring the public statement of the U.S. position on the legal
questions involved.
goolar

STANBARD FORM NO. 54
TO
: E - Mr. Nitze
DATE: Ootober 18, 1948
FROM : ITP - Mr. Martin
OFD - Mr. Knapp
SUBJECT:
SCAP's Labor Position.
I. Recommendation
We recommend that the State Department obtain Labor and Army Department
approval for the issuance of the following interim directive to SCAP as
a matter of urgency:
"Japanese Government employees, with the exception of
guardians of public safety and order such as police, firemen,
etc., and administrative employees normally included in civil
894.
service categories, should be given full exercise of the
normal trade union right to organize and bargain collectively,
but not the right to strike.
50
"The above policy is supplementary to and does not
derogate from the policy direction contained in JCS 63."
II. Discussion
The above action is justified, we believe, by the following
considerations:
a riguisicant parlur
-1848
a. There are approximately 2.5 million workers in Japan
employed by the Government. They constitute about 40% of the 6.6 million
workers who have been organized into unions under the terms of United
Stetes Government and FEC directives. Leeeathan 1million of these would
be considered normal civil servants or defenders of public safety in the
United States. The intent of FEC O45/5 (Principles for Japanese Trade
Unions) was to encourage the organization of workers into unions, and
to assist them in reserving and improving conditions of work and in
order that they might participate in the building up of a peaceful and
democratic Japan. To exclude all government employees from the exercise
of normal trade union rights seems to deny, without reason, to large
mumbers of Japanese workers the right to participate in these objectives.
b. While the FEC is not of one mind regarding the application
of FEC O45/5 to government employees, the Anglo-American countries, on
grounds of policy as well as on established traditions in their own
countries, have found objections to the exclusion of government employees
from the enjoyment of normal rights of labor. If their views are dis-
regarded by SCAP the interest on the part of these countries in the success
of other aspects of the occupation program may be seriously weakened and
their cooperation withheld. It should be borne in mind in this conneotion
that the

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
that the three British Empire Governments most concerned are labor
governments who are directly answerable to parliaments controlled by
labor members.
c. The language of FEC O45/5 is ambiguous and is possible
of being used to justify the position taken by SCAP that the directive
does not apply to government employees. On the other hand it can be
argued with equal power that neither the language nor FEC records justify
the exclusion of such workers from its terms. Such ambiguity is undesrable
and we believe, therefore, that a U.S. interim directive should be sent
to SCAP for the purpose of clarifying SCAP's present direotive.
d. Irrespective of the international implications of this
matter, we believe this interim directive is desirable on the ground of
the domestic situation in Japan. We believe the course of action SCAP
now proposes, which is to deny these trade union rights to large groups
of government industrial workers, will weaken the healthy demooratic
tendencies in the labor movement, strengthen the basis of open Communist
propoganda against the occupation, and contribtue to the subordination of
an important class of labor to the Japanese bureaucracy and, clandestinely,
to possible totalitarian leadership. The question might be raised here
as to why we feel the course of action we recommend will weaken rather
than strengthen the hold of communism on the labor movement. We believe
the most effective way to fight Communist influence in Japanese Government
enterprises is to give the workers involved as free an expression of
organizational rights as may be compatible with efficiency and public
order. It should be noted that SCAP has recognized this principle in his
plan that the government railroads be turned into public corporations
whose workers be allowed full organizational and the collective bargaining
rights as well as use of mediation and arbitration facilities. Under this
proposal, they will, therefore, be given exactly those organizational
rights we are desirous of having extended to other non-administrative
government employees. Promise of this development seems to us to have
forestalled a drift to the left among railroad workers. In contrast the
communication workers, to whom SCAP does not contemplate providing these
rights, have swung still further to the left since July 22. We believe
the extension of these same rights, coupled with denial of the use of
the strike or any other "dispute tactic" to all "industrial" employees of
government, to be the most effective antidote to Communist infiltration
and propaganda among this important group of workers.
e. There is no question that issuance of an interim directive
will in SCAP's opinion affect his prestige and the efficiency of the
occupation. To a considerable extent the effectiveness of MacArthur's
leadership depends, he believes, upon the consistency and logic of the
course he has followed. We do not believe that the course we recommend
need necessarily affect his prestige since the directive does not deal
w1th

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
with the applicability of JCS 63 itself to govermment employees, but
merely supplements that directive. It merely extends labor rights to
a wider category of industrial employees than recommended in the 22 July
letter and does not touch upon the recommendations as far as civil servants
are concerned.Moreover, the fact that the program we are recommending is
a "right" program, and much of MacArthur's support in Japan has been due
to its "rightness" in the past, we believe that the loss to his prestige
caused by what may be regarded as a superficial inconsistency in his
exercise of authority would more than outweigh the mass approval tob
expected in Japan of new policy direction from which large mumbers of
Japanese workers would obtain definite, though circumscribed, advantages.
We feel, further, that the effectiveness of U.S. leadership in Japan
should not rest upon any assumption, by the Japanese people, of its
infallibility. Domestic leadership can admit and correct error.
f. Two other considerations of great importance within the
United States itself, need to be noted. First, the United States
Department of Labor has taken a firm stand that the denial of the trade
union rights as proposed by SCAP is contrary to American practices for
similar workers in the United States and that that denial should not be
approved by the U.S. Government. Second, United States labor organizations
have become informed regarding SCAP's position, are alive to its
implications and are united in opposing it. In this latter connection the
position of the U.S. Government vis a vis SCAP can have far-reaching
implications in the domestic political situation which should not be
ignored.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
THE LEGAL ADVISER
October 19, 1948
TO:
0 - Mr. Claxton
FROM:
L/P - Conrad E. Snow
SUBJECT:
The Application of JC Serial Number 63 to Japanese
Government Employees
1. A legal opinion is desired as to the meaning of the
phrase "Japanese workers" in JC Serial #63 (FEC-045/6), or in
particular, as to whether the words include employees of the
Japanese Government.
2. JCR #63 provides in brief thet "Japanese workers"
8
should be encouraged to form themselves into trade unions; that
b
the right of trade unions and their members to organize should
f
be protected by law, and the freedem of "workers" to join trade
unions be provided for by law; that trade unions shall be
encouraged "to negotiate with the employers on behalf of their
力
members regarding terms and conditions of employment"; that the
Japanese Government should establish mediation and arbitration
o
machinery for deeling with industrial disputes that cannot be
settled by negotiation; and that strikes and other work stonpages
-1948
should be prohibited only when the occupation authorities consider
thet such stoppages would directly prejudice the objectives or
needs of the occuupation. The paper contains no reference to em-
ployees of the Government as such, and no limitation of the phrese
"Japanese workers" to non-governmental emnloyees.
3. Examination of the legislative history of JC #63 indicates
that its framers meant to include in the words "Jananese workers"
at least some categories of government employees.
a) The earliest antecedent of JC #63 was SWNCC-92/1,
adopted by State-War-Navy Coordinating Committee 28 Dec 45, and
circulated to the Joint Chiefs of Staff and to the American
representative on the FEC. The paper was entitled "Treatment
of 'Japanese Viorkers Organization". The paper was designed
"to determine the policy to be followed by the occupation
authorities in dealing with organizations of Japanese workers".
It is clear that the words "Japanese workers" in this paper
were intended to cover at least certain classes of government
employees. This is indicated by the following passages:
(1) In

=== Page 120 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
(1) In presenting the historical background, in the
"Discussion" (Appendix, p. 10), it was stated that "The
basic industries, such as communications, transport,
metals and chemicals, were more highly organized than
industry as a whole. Thus at one time forty percent
of the transport workers . . . were in unions". The
communications and transport workers were government
employees.
(2) In the discussion of labor legislation (Appendix
p. 13-14) the "Labor Disputes Adjustment Act of 1925"
(actually 1926) "under which arbitration of disputes
in public utilities and government industries was to be
compulsory", was stated to be of no practical significance,
because only four cases of arbitrations had been reported,
most disputes having been settled by police intervention.
This act actually covered several categories of government
employees.
(3) The conclusion was reached (p. 5), among others, that
the occupation authorities, in their own employment and
labor policies, should, when conditions warrant, give
positive encouragement to the development of labor organ-
izations. If the paper contemplated labor organisations
among the employees of the occupation authorities, a fortiori
it must have contemplated the same among employees of the
Japanese Government.
b) The first draft of the FEC policy paper (FEC-O39) was
submitted by the U.S. delegation on 28 March 1946, and closely
followed the SWNCC paper. The title "Treatment of Japanese
Workers' Organizations" was retained, and the first paragraph
provided that the Japanese Government should issue a general
proclamation recognizing the feedom of industrial and agricul-
tural workers to join trade unions. The conclusion of
SWNCC-92/1, as to the duty of the occupation authorities with
respect to the development of labor organizations, in their
own employment and labor policies, reappears as paragraph 3
of FEC-O39. Naturally, however, the matter contained in the
"Discussion" of SWNCC-92/1, did not appear in the FEC paper.
c) FEC-O39 was referred to Committee Number 4 of the FEC
and there reappeared in draft as C4-009/1. On 2 July 1946
appeared a new draft (C4-009/2), in which the title is changed to
"Principles for Japanese Trade Unions", and in which the words
"Japanese Workers" appear in the first paragraph, in the same
context as in FEC-045/6.
a) On

=== Page 121 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
d) On 10 July 1946, the U.S. member of Committee Number 4,
in response to requests for information, submitted a compilation
of actions taken by the Supreme Commander "to implement the
principles set forth in the U.S. directive on Japanese Workers'
Organization contained in FEC-O39". Thet the phrase "Japanese
Workera" was still conceived to include government employees is
indicated by the following passages:
(1) As to paragraph 3 of F C-O39, the U.S. member pointed
out that SCAP, in a letter to the Armies, subject Civilian
Labor Employed by Occupation Forces", dated 17 Nov 1945, had
stated that "the freedom of industrial and agricultural
workers to establish and to join democratic and orderly labor
association or trade unions for the purpose of bargaining
collectively with private employees, official and semi-
official companies, and government industries and services,
will be recognized."
(2) In the same connection he reported that instructions
had been issued guaranteeing "the right of employees working
for the Occupation Forces to organize and bargain collectively."
(3) With reference to strikes and other work stoppages the
same letter to the Armies had prohibited "strikes, lock-outs,
or other work stoppages which are inimical to the objectives
of military occupation". The U.S. member pointed out that
"in practice, transportation, communication, coal mining,
repatriation services and occupation forces' projects, have
been considered essential," and that this limitation, communi-
cated orally to the Japanese Government, had prevented several
serious stoppages in those categories.
e) In the Steering Committee the paper appeared as SC-032,
SC-032/1, SC-032/2, and SC-032/3, without change in the words in
question. It was passed by the Far Eastern Commission in the
same words, FEC-045/5, 9 December 1946, and sent to SCAP as a
U.S. Directive, FEC-045/6, 30 December 1946. Nothing in the
reported discussions in the Steering Commission or before the
Far Eastern Commission appears to bear on the interpretation in
cuestion.

POINTS AT ISSUE BETWEEN GENERAL
MACARTHUR AND STATE-LABOR-ARMY ON
JAPANESE GOVERNMENTAL LABOR.
2 of Oct 8and 12, 1948, th date of
State-Labor-Army concerning Japanese goveramental labor revelve 
about three basic issues, (a) right to strike, (b) self-orgamisation,
and (c) collective bargaining.
(a) Right to atrike. General MacArthur desires a law deoreeing no
strikes om the part of govermmental employees, whether "industrial"
employees (such as the equivalent of New York City's subway workers)
or "ordinary" (Civil Service white collar) employees.
State-Labor-Army believe that under present conditions in Japan
no strikes should be permitted, but do not desire to see a mo-striks
provision embodied in law, rather, they desire SCAP to make a finding
that strikes and other work stoppages on the part of any and all alasses
of Government employees would directly prejudice the objectives and
meeds of the dccupation, such finding to be inaluded in any statement
made prior to the enactment of the general Civil Service legislation or
at the time of its approval by SCAP. This would be in effect a no-strike
provision for the duration of occupation, without prejudicing the choice
which the Japanese people may decide to make in this field after the end
of the occupation.
(b) Self-organization. With respect to both "industrial" and
"ordinary" governmental employees, General MacArthur would limit the
choice of representatives to persons who are working within the Japanese
governmental service; these persons would be permitted to spend only a
small part of their time performing union work, the amoumt of time
being fixed by the National Personnel Authority, whieh would be the
Japamese equivelent of the Civil Service Commission, but with greatly
expanded powers.
State-Labor-Atmy take the position that both "industrial" and
"ordinary" governmental enployees should be entitled to have repre-
sentatives of their own choosing bargain for them, including full-
time, paid persons if so desired. Abuses by way of exclusive time
spent on union activity, etc., could be regulated by the Natiomal
Personnel Authority.
(c) For both

-2
(e) For both "industrial" and "ordinary" govermmestal
employees, General Maekrthur would Limit eallestive bargeining to

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
TELEPHONE
DATE: October 25, 1948
SUBJECT:
Japanese Employment Abroad
PARTICIPANTS: Mr. Tswen-ling Tsui, Counsellor, Chinese Embassy

Mr. Tsui telephoned to reply to questions asked in previous conversations
of September 17, 1948 (as summarized in Memorandum of September 20, 1948,
2
previously circulated) concerning the Chinese request to SCAP for approval of
5
the,employment of 20 Japanese technicians to re-install a paper mill at Canton.
48
Mr. Tsui stated that upon reporting the previous conversations to his
Ambassador, he had been instructed to inform the Department that the above
request did not reflect any.change of policy on the part of his Government
or any change in its attitude towards the travel of Japanese abroad. He said
that the Chinese Government considered the proposed travel and employment of
these Japanese technicians to be an action flowing from the past circumstances
of the Japanese looting of the mill in question and its subsequent restitution
to China. The proposals involving Japanese travel currently under consideration
by the Commission dealt, on the other hand, with the future. The latter ques-
tions are regarded by the Chinese Government as clearly distinguishable from
CS/A
the former and as meriting separate and possibly different consideration.
Mr. Tsui was thanked for his response to our enquiry (in the form of a
lesson in Chinese logic) and was assured that the views of his Government
would be noted by the interested officers of the Department.

TO
: O - Mr. Saltzman
DATE: 0ctober 27, 1948
SUBJECT:
Draft Radio to SCAP On The National Publio Servioe Law
Part II (less its Paragraph B) of the accompanying
Draft Radio to SCAP on the National Public Servioe Law is
not regarded as acceptable for a number of the reasons
whioh I inoluded in my memorandum to you of Ootober 2D,
as well as for the following reasons:
1. SCAp 1s by his own admission deeply committed on
the present draft of the NPSL. This draft bears the imprint
of long and hard negotiation not only as between SCap and
the Ashida Cabinet, but between the various Japanese political
parties. The attaohed Draft Radio is asking that substantive
changes be made in what is probably the most important para-
graph in the NPSL, that 1e, Article 98. Aocording to the
Octcber 1 Intelligence Survey (Secret), "Protracted discussions
on the Draft NPSL have now resulted in a practical compromise
between the Government and...the left-wing of the Sooial
Democratio Party. The Government succeeded in winning its
point on Article 98, but lost out on the classifioation of
employees contained in Article 13 of the accompanying Regu-
lations...Communist protests will be vooiferous..." To
f
direct SCAP to make substantive changes 1n Paragraph 98
will evidently upset the balance on which the present com-
promise Draft has been accepted, requiring an indefinite
力
period of renegotiation.
Following the acoeptance of the present Draft NPSL by
the Ashida Cabinet, a more conservative regime has come to
power. As SCAP strongly emphasized in his C-64695, 1t would
be most improbable that the Yoshida Cabinet would seek to
liberalize the NPSL in the direotion suggested by Part II A
of this Radio. Because it would be a clear indication to
the world at large that he had been overruled by the U. S.
Government on the NPSL, SCAP might be inclined to acoede to
present requests that there be an immediate dissolution of
the Diet. This would postpone the enactment of vital legis-
lation and would continue the Interim Cabinet Ordinance 201
in effect indefinitely.
2. We have not yet received from SCAP a full statement
of his reasons for opposing Paragraph 8 of War 90494. Undoubt-
edly he has in mind the thought that every precaution should
be
SECRET

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
-2
be taken to insure that the Japanese Government remain
free from Communist infiltration, which might be facilitated
by the membership of Government workers in unions dominated
by the Japanese Communist Party. The suggestion is advanced
in the attached Radio thet the Law should permit Government
employees to join employee orgenizations of their own
choosing, but that the National Personnel Authority have
authority to adopt regulations to prevent abuses of this
right. However, regulations to prevent abuses of this
right could, seemingly, not be wholly effective unless they
went so far as virtually to say by regulation what is now
said by law. It would be more direct, honest and a better
precedent for the future if the true meaning of this para-
graph were set forth clearly in the Law, rather than per-
mitting the Law to say one thing and the internal regulations
another. This would also be more consistent with out efforts
to encourage the Japanese to govern by legislative process
rather than by the orders and regulations which were so
cheracteristic of authoritarian government in Japan in the
prewar years.
3. It may be argued that the proposed Radio only
requires that SCAP make minor changes in the Draft Law.
Aside from the probability thst SCAp would not agree that
the proposed changes were minor, I think it is much wiser
from this Government's point of view, having fully acquainted
SCAP of 1ts views in War 90494, to let SCAP take the full
responsibility for not having availed himself of these views.
There is bound to be widespread controversy and labor unrest
in Japan connected with this legislation. If we force SCAP
to make certain "minor" amendments in the Law, we may be
assured that whatever goes wrong in Japen thereafter will
be attributed by SCAP and by his supporters in Japan and
the United States to the fact that SCAp was overruled on
this point by the U.S. Government. It would seem to be a
far sounder tactic to tell SCAP at this stage that we must
rely on nis judgment in this matter end thus cause SCAp to
take full responsibility for the consequences.
It was my understanding that this was the decision teken
in your office last week and that this wes to be the State
Department's position.

Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: Nbvember 1, 1948
SUBJECT:
Labor Policy in Japan
References:
1 - State-Labor-Army Radio to SCAP 10/8/48
3 - E Memo
2 - SCAP Radio 10/12/48
4 - L Memo
PARTICIPANTS:
5 - FE Memo
0
E
Mr. Saltzman
Mr. Nitze
L
FE
894.504/11-14
Mr. Wilds
Mr. Barnett
Mr. Tate

Mr. Claxton

The meeting was held on Thursday, October 21, 1948 in the office of the
1-1493
48
Assistant Secretary of State for Occupied Areas.
Mr. Saltzman opened the meeting by noting that the FE memorandum (reference
5) indicated disagreement with the views and recommendations presented in the E
memorandum (reference 3), and by asking Mr. Nitze whether the U. S. Government
has to take action with respect to the pending and proposed labor legislation
for Japan.
Mr. Nitze replied that in his view no philosophical question of whether or
not U. S. Government "had" to take action was involved, but rather whether or not
the U. S. Government felt it desirable to show a policy interest in Japanese labor
matters. He stated that his office had no doubt that the U. S. Government would
have a political and economic policy interest in the fate of the labor movement
CS/A
in Japan. Whether or not Japanese labor in general should be encouraged to
organize itself along democratic lines did not seem to be at issue, and he did
not regard the legal argument for the application of FEC O45/5 to government
workers as a major factor in the views of his office (reference 3). Th  were,
however, three fundamental issues of economic principle involved in the cent
developments of the SCAP program for government workers -- (a) their right to
free association and organization, and to representation of their owm chosing,
(b) their right to collective bargaining, mediation and arbitration, and)
4.5
should, during the period of occupation, be allowed the right to strike,mnd
his knowledge no one had proposed that they should have this right. Thuhere
were really only two issues. Secondly, except for certain groups such a the
2
police
SECRET
5586

=== Page 128 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-2-
police and other guardians of public safety, and the normal civil service for
whom other provisions were made, he considered it universally agreed that all
other government workers should have the right to collective bargaining. It
appeared to E that it is clearly important to U. S. foreign economic policy to
recognize the claims of all Japanese government workers to the first right of
free organization and of representation of their owm choosing, and of all but
the two excepted groups to the right of collective bargaining. In this con-
nection, in view of the withdrawal of the right to strike, he felt that final
arbitration should be provided as a substitute.
Mr. Saltzman asked whether E felt that SCAP should be directed to grant
these rights to the indicated groups, despite his sharp representations to the
contrary (reference 2) based on conditions in Japan with which he was more
familiar than the groups considering the problem in Washington.
Mr. Butterworth stated that he did not think SCAP should be directed against
his judgment in the matter.
r. Nitze said he did not personally favor going so far as to issue an in-
terim directive on this subject, but felt the principle set forth was a right
principle and an important one, and that SCAP should be informed in the most
firm, clear, and definite terms that the U. S. Government expected him to abide
by that principle in his relations with the Japanese Government.
Mr. Saltzman commented that this had already been done. SCAP had, however,
ignored our views on the basis of his knowledge of the situation. He added that
SCAP had every right to exercise his own judgment until and unless he was other-
wise directed by proper authority, as established for the present regime of control.
Mr. Nitze stated that E considered the principles of economic policy in-
volved in this situation so important that the U. S. Government should be assured
that its views were made unmistakeably clear to SCAP,. and that these views could
be more firmly stated than in the last message (reference 1).
Mr. Saltzman proposed that a message be sent to SCAP which would (a) reiterate
the support of the U. S. Government for the policy views and recommendations of
the previous message as those which this government regards as generally applicable
and as appropriate goals to be sought in Japan under normal conditions, (b) recog-
nize SCAP's views and reasons for objecting, under present circumstances as he
described them, to implementing these policies at this time, (c) accept SCap's
judgment on the matter as not to be formally challenged by the U. S. Government
sitting at a distance from the situation in Japan itself.
Mr. Claxton commented that the position of E and FE did not appear to be so
far apart. Although the FE memorandum (reference 5) did not address itself to
the legal question of the applicability of FEC O45/5 to government workers as
did the L memorandum (reference 4) nor to what ir. Nitze had called the first
right, of free association, organization and representation (to which the Depart-
ment of Labor also attached great importance) FE had suggested that if commonly
and promptly
SECRET

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-3-
and promptly acceptable to the U. S. Government agencies concerned, it should be
proposed that the telecommunications workers should be formed into a government
corporation which would presumably entitle them to the rights of mediation and
arbitration. This suggestion would also conform to the opinion of L (reference 4).
As a matter of fact, r. Claxton added, the Under Secretary of the Army already
sent a personal message to SCAP making this proposal as a compromise solution of
the controversy that had arisen.
Mr. Butterworth said if this had been done, there was little point to the
present meeting, as the entire issue had been compromised. He had no doubt of
SCAP's reply to such a message.
Mr. Saltzman referred to his previously suggested outline of a message to
SCAP, and asked for reactions of FE, L and E.
Mr. Butterworth indicated his agreement with its substance.
Mr. Tate stated that his office, upon carefully reviewing the legislative
history of FEC O45/5, had concluded that at least certain government workers,
such as transport and communications workers, were clearly intended to be covered
by the policy decision, although the language of the directive itself was unclear.
He said he was aware that the contrary view, that the policy decision had no
bearing on government workers, had been expressed publicly by the Political Adviser
to SCAP and privately by SCAP himself but flet that this latter view was not sup-
ported by its history.
Mr. Saltzman asked whether if the language of the decision was obscure, there
was any reason to expect SCAP to be familiar with or governed by its history, rather
than by his own interpretation of the directive he had received.
General Snow noted that the documents which L had reviewed in arriving at
its.opinion had also been available to SCAP and that SCAP had himself referred
(in reference 2) to the deliberetions leading to the adoption of the policy. He
pointed out that legally, the obscurity of the language of FEC O45/5 could be
corrected by a new decision, or by a supplemental directive interpreting the one
previously sent to SCAP.
Mr. Saltzman commented that since the language of FEC O45/5 was general or
obscure, and since there seemed to be a clear division of opinion in the FEC on
the applicability of FEC O45/5 to Government vo rkers SCAP would seem within his
rights to give the directive his executive interpretation, as in the case of all
other directives, and asked again for L's views on the outline of the message to
SCAP which he had proposed.
Mr. Tate stated that L had confined its study to the legal aspects of the
problem, that its findings were that the legal theory underlying the proposed
message was the least tenable one, but that L's function did not include considera-
tion of the political aspects of the problem.
Mr. Nitze
SECRET

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-4-
Mr. Nitze then stated that on the substance of the problem, the question of
the labor relations program to be followed in Japan in furtherance of U. S. foreign
economic policy interests, he did not believe there were any different positions
being taken by the offices present. He said that E considered the principles in-
volved to be those set forth in the previous message to Scap (reference 1) and
rephrased in the E memorandum (reference 3). These principles should, he felt,
be supported by the U. S. Government and restated to SCAP for application in Japan,
unless there were countervailing political considerations which required subordina-
tion of economic policy. Whether there were such countervailing political considera-
tions was for FE to say.
Mr. Saltzman asked whether ir. Nitze wished the problem presented to the
Acting Secretary for decision.
Mr. Nitze considered this not necessary, and commented that from the position
FE had taken, he assumed that there were countervailing political considerations.
Mr. Butterworth indicated that there were such considerations.
Mr. Saltzman stated nis understanding that the offices represented concurred
in the substance of the message to SCAP which he had earlier proposed in outline.
Mr. Sullivan asked the meeting to consider that the probable consequences of
despatching the proposed message to SCAP would include: (1) objections from the
Department of Labor which might be carried to the Cabinet level for resolution,
(2) the disapproval of the U. S. labor movement, which might be reflected in its
attitude towards other foreign economic programs of the Government, (3) adverse
reactions of FEC members who would feel that their vlews had been disregarded,
that the U. S. Government had not discharged its commitment to review the pending
Japanese legislation in the light of the views they had expressed, and that SCAP
and the U. S. Government had acted contrary to the political interests of these
FEC countries in dealing with domestic and international labor movements, and (4)
adverse conditions for the conduct of SCAP's economic program in Japan. He asked
that these potential consequences be weighed before final decision was reached.
Mr. Barnett commented that r. Sullivan's remarks constituted essentially
political views which supported the taking of a positive stand by the U. S. Govern-
ment with respect to SCAP's program for government workers in Japan. He suggested
that a relevant topic for consideration was a review of the countervailing political
considerations which FE had in mind, and which he had not yet heard.
Mr. Green mentioned the adverse effect on SCAP's prestige which would result
from failure of the U. S. Government to support him fully in the program he had
worked out with the Japanese Government.
Mr. Nitze
SECRET
798

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.97)
SECRET
-5-
Mr. Nitze stated that he did not consider the protection of scap's prestig
in his relationship with the U. S. Government to be the sort of important politio
consideration that would be countervailing.
Mr. Green said that, of course, more significance should be attached to the
rejection of SCap's judgment that would be involved in pressing a contrary cours
of action on him, than to the question of prestige.
Mr. Saltzman closed the meeting by restating his understanding that the
ranking representatives of FE, E and L had concurred in the substance of the
proposed message to SCAP.

CONFIDENTIAL
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ASSISTANT SECRETARY
November 24, 1948
O - Mr. Saltzman
There is attached a memorandum of conversation with Mr. Graves of the
British mbassy which departmental officers had with him following the FEC
meeting this morning at which he made a statement (also attached) on Japanese
National Public Service Law.
After the conversation with ir. Graves the entire problem of handling
the government labor matter in the FC was discussed by Messrs. Schuler,
Green, Blakeslee and myself with General McCoy. You will recall this question
is formally before the Commission on the basis of a Soviet resolution which
would have the FEC criticize SCAP's letter of July 22 and the interim action
of the Japanese cabinet. The majority of the Conmission members have indicated
that they will oppose the Soviet resolution or at least will not support it,
and that their real interest is in the permanent legislation. It would seem
possible to obtain a vote on the Soviet resolution which would cause it to
fail of passage and remove it from the agenda. However, this would still
leave the much more difficult problem of permanent legislation. Thus far no
statements have been made relating specifically to the permanent legislation
which would cause it to be formally before the Commission, except the UK state-
ment attached.
e regard the only danger in adhering to this matter, but a serious one,
as the possibility that some member of the Commission--perhaps the Soviets or
the British--will propose a revision of FEC-O45/5, the paper dealing with labor
policy in Japan, or an entirely new policy paper which would provide as a
matter of policy that government workers should have some rights not presently
bargaining and/or the right of appeal to an independent arbitof tribunal.
X
Should this occur it would seem quite possible that a large majority of the
29
members would feel a strong obligation to vote for such a paper. Should the
paper be proposed by the USSR it would be enbarrassing to all concerned but
particularly to the U.S., since it might well develop that the other nations
would feel in this case obligated to support the Soviet resolution.
As you will see from the attached memorandum of conversation with
ir. Graves, he has given no indication the UK will go so far as to propose such
a new policy. The actions of the other members, particularly the USSR, cannot
be predicted.
Under the circumstances it seems necessary for the U. S. to make a clear
X
decision, either to accept the possibility of such a proposal being made by some
other country and the ultimate development of the U. S. being placed in a
minority
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.85)
CONFIDENTIAL
-2-
minority position which might be particularly embarrassing if it were the
Soviet member who put forwerd the proposal, or of taking the initiative
either directly or by agreement with some other member--perhaps the Common-
wealth member--to put forward the minimum modification of FEC-O45/5 which
would meet the desires of the Commonwealth members.
I believe this matter should be fully considered within the government
and a conclusion reached on it before the next meeting of the Commission.
2
P. F. Claxton, Jr.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: February 21, 1949
SUBJECT:
Labor Situation in Japan, Korea and Okinawa
Mr. Gibson, Assistaut Secretary, Department of Labor
Mr. Rusk
1940
Mr. Butterworth
Mr. Green (NA)
COPIES TO:
U
S568
G
O
E
FE(3)
POLAD, Tokyo and AIREP, Seoul
8
6
94.50
At my invitation, Mr. Gibson called at the Department to present
his views on the labor situation in Japan, Korea and Okinawa following
his recent trip there.
an
ir. Gibson said that on the basis of conversations with many
2
Japanese trade union and management groups, as well as with Headquarter
officials, he had comeaway with the decided impression that the labor
2
situation in Japan is not all that it might be. In his opinion, the
trend of occupation labor policies to date had been characterized first
4
by an overly liberal extension of rights and privileges to an inexper-
ienced labor movement, followed, in the summer of 1948, by an abrupt
shift of policy which is going too far in the other direction. This
he regarded as the basis for the present growth of Communism in the
trade union movement.
Mr. Gibson continued as followa: occupation officials are prone,
0
in explaining the January elections, to dismiss the Communist upsurge
8
as a passing Japanese reaction to the ineptitude and corruption of
9
recent governments and to the Chinese Communist victories. They appar- 
8
ently fail to see in the election returns a warning that positive
2
S
5
measuresI
C
ut
CONFIDENTIAL
2
-2149

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
- 2 -
measures are called for in combatting Communism, particularly in
intellectual and trade union circles where it presents its greatest
threat. One of the most effective ways of meeting the situation,
he thought, would be sending as soon as possible a committee of top
U.S. union leaders to Japan to give the Japanese some idea of how
democratic trade unions function in the United Stetes. This would
further provide much-needed advice and moral support to the Democ-
ratization League which is now unassisted in its struggle against
Communism in unions. Arriving at a time when the whole labor
movement in Japan is in a ferment over the projected revision of
the Trade Union and Labor Relations Adjustment Law at the forthcoming
Diet session, such a committee would exert a moderating effect both
on the attitudes of the unione towards needed changes in the labor
lawe and on the drafters of the laws themselves. In this connection
he suggested that it might be advantageous if a labor attache were
assigned to USPOLAD.
Mr. Gibson suggested additional ways in which the labor situa-
tion could be improved, es followa: (1) in the democratization of
the labor movement, the Occupation should place lese emphasis on
laws and more emphasis on education and guidance; (2) indirect
measures should be taken to remove the Communists who are now
members of the Central Labor Relations Board; and (3) employers
chould no longer pay "employees" who work full time as union repre-
sentatives.
Mr. Gibson seid that in discussing the National Publio Service
Law with General MacArthur, the General claimed to have no expert
knowledge of the labor and civil service technicalities involved and
to have acted on the advice given him by Headquarters specialists.
Mr. Gibson added that he could only conclude that the National Public
Service Lew was the handiwork of one perhaps biased group in Head-
quarters. In his opinion, the Law's shortcomings were: (1) its
penal provisions which he considered excessive, (2) the fact that it
increased in effect portal-to-portal hours for almost all government
workers by ten to twenty hours, (3) its failure to reorganize the
Communications Ministry, or part of it, into a government corporation
as had been done in the case of the government railways and monopolies.
Mr. Gibson stated that he had received assurance from General
MacArthur that henceforth all projected Japaneee labor legis-
lation would be submitted to the
Department
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 142 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
- 3 -
Department of Labor for review and comment prior to enactment; that
henceforth the Labor Section of Headquarters, not the Government
Section, would assist in the promotion of labor legislation through
the Diet.
The Japanese coal industry represented in Mr. Gibson's opinion
almost the ultimate in inefficiency. Equipment and methods are
below standards prevailing in the U.S. at the turn of the century.
There are far too many workers above ground in relation to those
working at the coal-face. Featherbeading, bad everywhere in Japan,
is at its worst in this heavily subsidized industry. Mr. Gibson
concluded that a great deal more could be accomplished under the
a
occupation in stepping up vital coal and hydro-electric output.
Mr. Butterworth inquired whether, in view of the traditional paternal-
ism of Japanese management, it would be possible appreciably to
reduce the number of unneeded workers. Mr. Gibson replied that
this problem could be partially met through proper unemployment
compensation and public works programs, but that little progress
had so far been made in this direction by the Occupation.
Mr. Gibson stated that both employer and employee groups fully
support the stabilization program.  He believed that some of the
strongest support would continue to come from the labor unions
provided the program is administered equitably. He regarded a
minimum wage scale as important despite rising costs.
In Korea, Mr. Gibson saw no signs of a free labor movement:
what unions there are seemed to him quite political in purpose.
He attributed this situation largely to the present deep-seated,
though understandable, fear of Communist penetration into organized
labor. Only 4 or 5 trade union agreements exist at present. Mr.
Gibson expressed the view that it is imperative to modernize the
Korean coal mining industry, which he regarded as even more back-
ward and inefficient than the Japanese. I suggested the application
of "point 4" in bringing this about. Mr. Gibson agreed and added
that with the further supplying of about 3.5 million dollars of
coal mining equipment the Korean coal industry could be satisfactorily
improved. Mr. Gibson further stressed the need for a good U.S.
labor adviser in Korea. He also mentioned the need for expediting
the fertilizer contracts for Korea.
In
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 143 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIAL
-4 -
In Okinawa, Mr. Gibson thought that immediate steps should
be taken to correct the present system of wages whereby Ryukyuan
laborers working for the U.S. Army are paid only $14 per month as
compared to prevailing monthly wage rates there of $48, with the
difference being largely made up by handouts of PX supplies.
Mr. Rusk expressed the hope that Mr. Gibson would commit to
paper these and any other recommendations he would care to make on
the Japanese, Korean and Okinawan labor situation, in order that
the State Department and other U.S. agencies concerned might have
an opportunity to give them careful study. Mr. Gibson indicated
that he would do so.
I thanked Mr. Gibson for his illuminating discussion and
expressed the hope that the State and Labor Departments would
cooperate closely on this and all other matters of common interest.

894.504
This meeting was called in order to acquaint interested
Department officers vith the results of r. Gibson's trip to Japan
in February and with his conclusions on recent developments affect-
ing the Japanese labor situation.
Ir. Cibson began his ciscussion by describing the development
of the labor movement unier the ccupation and made the following
major comments on the present situation:
1. About 0 percent of the top union leadership are either
23
Communists or Comnunist-sympathizers. Yet the Covernment has brought
these leaders into government posts as labor representatives on
mediation boards,largely ignoring the appointment of moderate leaders.
30588
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
a
2
2. The inauguration of the 48-hour work week for government workers
shortly before the January election was a significant factor in driving
government workers to support the Communists. The recent swing to the
left in Japan was the result of the support of the Communist Party not
only by government workers, but also by educators and small shop-owmers.
It is possible that the Communists might develop into a potent force in
Japan and conceivably take over the Government at a general election,
perhaps four years hence.
3. The Yoshida Covernment is undoubtedly aiming to throttle the
Japanese labor movement curing the present Diet session. It is believed
that the Yoshida Government is purposely doing little to curb the spread
of Communism with the hope that Comrunist activities will become so
obnoxious that the Government would have no difficulty in securing Diet
acceptance to restrictive labor legislation.
In answer to Mr. Saltzman's inquiry about the attitude of unions
other than those of government workers towards the National Public Ser-
vice Law, Mr. Gibson replied that there was' opposition to the law by
all unions because to them it represented (1) the first move to curb
the gains macde by labor since the end of the war and (2) an anti-union
attitude on the part of SCAP.
Mr. Sullivan asked what were the points in the NPSL, other than
its penal provisions, to which the unions were opposed. Mr. Gibson
mentioned the outlawing of collective bargaining and the right to
strike, although, ne said, there was a feeling that the unions would
accept a ban on strikes if collective bargaining and compulsory
arbitration were set up instead. He added that there was some mention
made in Japan that the NPSL was in violation of the Japanese constitution.
(Act 28 of the Constitution states: "The right of workers to organize
and to bargain and act collectively is guaranteed.")
Mr. Parnett asked if Japanese labor leaders regarded SCAP or
the Japanese conservatives as being responsible for the NPSL. Mr.
Gibson said that they consider both to be responsible, but place most
of the blame on the SCAP top staff. ir. Earnett inquired further
whether SCAP intervention in the revisions of labor legislation that
are contemplated by the Yoshida Covernment would embarass SCAP or
increase SCAP's prestige among labor leaders. Lir. Gibson replied that
such intervention would enhance SCAP's prestige, and, furthermore, if
SCAP should reverse itself and require the Japanese Government to reduce
the work week to 40 hours for government workers, SCAP would gain much
popularity from this group. ir. Gibson added that the attitude of the
Labor Division had prevented more repressive amendments to Japanese
labor laws than were actually submitted.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 150 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
3
In reply to Mr. Sullivan's query whether General MacArthur was
fully acquainted with the NPSL, Mr. Cibson said that he felt the General
was not. Mr. Gibson said that there was some evidence that the SCAP
top staff had not divulged to General MacArthur the actual provisions
of the law. For example, General MacArthur's staff had said that there
were no penal provisions in the law while Mr. Gibson was able to point
out these provisions to the General and analyze their implications for
him.
Mr. Parnett inquired if the achievement of wage stabilization in
Japan would result in more effective utilization of Japanese labor or
in widespread unemployment. fr. Gibson said that he had seen little
evidence that Japanese industrialists were reducing their staffs to
eliminate the underemployment of their workers despite SCAP desires in
the matter. As yet the Japanese have not. developed real public works and
relief programs to take care of unemployment that would develop.
Mr. Allison asked what would be the attitude of American labor
towards American imports of Japanese goods on an increasing basis, re-
sulting from a need to maximize dollar exchange. Mr. Gibson replied that
the best way to reduce the prejudices of American labor would be to have a
group of American labor leaders go to Japan. Likewise some Japanese
labor leaders should come to the U.S. as soon as possible and meet with
American labor groups. He said that an American trade union mission to
Japan is now being planned and the idea had not encountered any opposition
from General macArthur.
The interchange of labor leaders is one of a series of recommend-
ations that Mr. Gibson is sending to General MacArthur at the latter's
request. ther recommendations that Mr. Gibson said he was making are:
1. The elimination of the appointment of Communists to govern-
ment labor mediation posts and the recognition of moderate labor leaders
by appointing them to these posts. Likewise the size of these labor
mediation groups shoula be reduced.
2. Immediate revision of the NPSL to eliminate its objectionable
features, as described above.
3. A minimum wage law which would fix a floor on wages at a
reasonable level.
4. Support the Democratization League which has three noteworthy
principles: (a) anti-communism; (b) anti-company unionism; and (c) cooper-
ation with management to increase production.
5. The person who will become top labor advisor for General
MacArthur should be a member of the American trade union mission to Japan.
He should not be appointed labor advisor until after he had gained
General MacArthur's confidence as a member of the mission. As labor
advisor he shoulu be attached to the Labor Division.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 151 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFiDENTIAL
4
6. If there are to be amendments to Japanese labor laws they
should be done as soon as possible because there have been some errors
made in the existing legislation and because while Occupation authority
exists in Japan, restrictive amencments can be curbed. Some of the spec-
ific amendments that might be made would be (1) to require all unions
to have by-laws and constitutions, (2) to establish election procedures
for choosing collective bargaining representatives, and (3) to require
democratic elections for filling union offices.

SUBJECT:
Mr. Barnett informed me last night that Mr. Bullock of the
Australian Delegation to the FEC has submitted to the joint
committee considering labor in Japan the following proposed
policy decision:
"The Far Eastern Commission, bearing in mind that 1
1s a matter for the occupation authorities in Japan to
decide when strikes and other work stoppages should be
prohibited as directly prejudicing the objectives or needs
of the occupation, decides as a matter of policy that the
provisions of FEC-O45/5 should be applied to workers in
Japanese government enterprises."
This proposed policy decision was supported enthusiastially
by all members except the United States, Philippines and the UsSR
said that his purpose in putting in a suggested policy paper
to the FEC was solely to attempt something constructive and
8
avoid harping criticism of the occupation. He also hoped thi
49
the Soviet tactics of forcing FEC members to vote against
proposals which they and their governnnts, at least in part,
favor could be circumvented. I had not then before me the
Australian proposal, but I did take occasion to point out
emphatically to Mr. Bullock what I considered to be some of
the practicalities of the situation. I emphasized that Japan
is a dynamic nation of 80 million people, with growing popula-
tion; that Japan would not remain static and would either seek
a better place in the world through cooperation and friendship
with the United States and like-minded nations or would move
toward and eventually join the Communist power system in an
effort to gain that better place; that we had to consider not
only economic and political conditions in Japan and their
control and development under the occupation, but also their
control and development by the Japenese leaders themselves after
the occupation is terminated; that we had been, if not foolish,
at least overzealous in destroying much of Japan's traditional
social relationships and had ignored Japanese experience in
handling the difficult problem of labor relations; that we had
gone much too far, in my opinion, in giving Japanese Communists
a golden opportunity to exploit Japanese labor by failing to
CS/H
inculcate

4.

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.99)

inculcate responsibilities along with extension of rights; and
that Australia, a white country nearby Asia which was rapidly
becoming Communist, should have the greatest apprehension re-
garding the possibilities that Japen might throw its destiny
into the Communiat power system.
I believe that before the question of the handling of this
labor problem gets entirely out of hand it would be desirable
to call in the representatives of the friendly FEC governments
and to talk to them frankly and firmly along the following
lines:
1. The United States from the outset took the initiative
and responsibility in bringing to Japan progressive reforms.
The United States has no intention of relinquishing its leader-
ship in that connection or of allowing the Japanese to abandon
the besic principles of those reforms.
2. The Japanese must be allowed ever-increasing measure
of responsibility and initiative as they proceed to assimilate
and adapt the basic principles of those reforms and accordingly
the Far Eastern Commisslon should avoid any attempt to dictate
the implementation or administration of reform measures in Japan.
The Japanese Public Service Law, just as any other law, is subject
to amendment by the Japsnese legislative process and the Far
Eastern Commission should allow this process full liberty and
freedom from interference.
3. The various FEC governments can communicate to SCAP
such views as they may have regarding the handling of the labor
problem in Japan, either through this government or perhaps
more appropriately through their representatives in Japan, but
they should bear in mind that the responeibility for handling
such difficult problems must remain in the hands of the authori-
ties in Japan, SCAP and the Japanese government. The United
States Government 1tself will make known to SCAP 1ts own views
regarding this difficult situation and will offer to SCAP and
to the Japanese Government all appropriate assistance to assure
that, in the light of prevailing conditions, there is the broadest
possible application of approved policies for the development
of free trade union movements within Japan not subject to Com-
munist infiltration and manipulation.
4. The FEC should decide that the basic principles regarding
labor in Japan were fully set forth in FEC O45/5, that the
1mplementation of those principles must be left to SCAP and the
Japanese Governnent and that those governments who wish to advise

At the luncheon this noon Mr. Gibson was shown the telegrams
from General MacArthur and Mr. Dod ge advising strongly against
the sending of an official U.s. labor mission to Japan at this
time. It was obvious that Mr. Gibson's initial reaction to
these telegrams was unfavorable. He immediately said that he
did not believe that we should rely solely on Genral MacArthur
and Mr. Dodge for an evaluation of the probable effect of sending
1-7
such a mission. He added that he felt that the labor situation
in Japan is critical and that a program should be developed at
22
89
the "grass roots" level to combat Communiem; that something
should be done to assure that the labor movement in Japan
4.504/4-1
developed favorably to the United States; that he would estimate
that at least 60 per cent of the top leadership of the labor
movement in Japan at the present time is Communist or Communist-
controlled; that the Department of Labor had explored rather
carefully the question of a mission to Japan and had in mind
a mission of perhape five "top flight" persons, including one
woman; and that he felt that to get a "grass roots" program
started in Japan it would be desirable to use such a miesion
for the springboard and to plan for a Japanese mission to the
United States.

6

S

W

who would be inclined to be more cautious at least at the
outset; and that he would appreciate it if Mr. Gibson would take

Mr. West pointed out that he felt very strongly that we
should not send an official labor mission to Japan in the face
of the objections which have been raised by General MacArthur
and Mr. Dodge.
Upon being asked what I thought of this problem, I emphasized
that the Department of State was very cognizant of the difficulties
which we face from the developing labor situation in Japan; that
we believe that something constructive must be done to correct
present unfortunate trends which are not only causing us diffi-
culties with other countries in the Far Eastern Commission but
are also detrimental to our position in Japan and are bringing
forth uncomplimentary statements from important sections of publio
opinion in this country. I added that the Department, however,
could not help but be impressed by the arguments which General
MacArthur and Mr. Dodge had put forth against the sending of an
official labor mission at this time; that we are all well aware
that conditions in Japen at present are turbulent and there is
a growing feeling of tenseness which could easily be further
aggravated or disturbed by the fanfare which would accompany the
sending of an official labor mission to Japan. Accordingly we
would be reluctant to push ehead the sending of an official
labor mission without considerable further exploration of this
problen with General MacArthur and Mr. Dodge, particularly
as we must of necessity rely to a large measure upon the evaluation
of the man on the spot in assessing the probable reaction to
a step of this sort. I referred again to the feeling in the
Department of State that something must be done in a constructive
way to improve the labor situation in Japan and suggested that
perhaps if the Department of Labor could select, and the Department
of the Aray send to Japan, one or more labor advisers who would
have our confidence as well as that of SCAP and who could imme-
diately set to work on the sort of "rass roots" program Mr. Gibson
had in mind the situation might be improved and the disturbing
elements which Mr. Dodge and General MacArthur envisaged avoided.
At the end of the luncheon Mr. Gihson said that he would
discuss the matter with Secretary Tobin and get in touch with
Mr. West. As I was leaving Mr. Voorhees emphasized that the
Department of the Army wanted to work as a team with State and
Labor on this matter and hoped tha: satisfactory arrangements
could be worked out.
After leaving the Pentagon Mr. Gibson emphasized to me the
great danger which he felt lay in the labor movement in Japan
if Communist influence were not offset and & real program to
win over the labor movement undertaken. He said his estimate
of 60 per cent Communist leadership was probably too small.
He expressed

which he had found in SCAp Headquarters regarding this problem.
I told Mr. Gibson that I would be inclined to share his appre-
hension regarding growing Communist influence and the potential
dangers of the labor movement in Japan under such influence
and that I too had found far too much complacenoy in SCAP
Headquarters when I was out there.

Proposed Policy Decision on Labor Situation in Japan
Submitted by Australian Government to FEC
Australian Inspection of Japanese Sodium Anhydride Process
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. H. W. Bullock, Second Secretary, Australian Embassy
Mr. J. M. Allison, Deputy Director, FE
