Mr. Bullock called by appointment on Mr. Allison this after-
6
noon. Mr. Allison said that before taking up the matter on
which he had asked Mr. Bullock to come in to see him he was
glad to be able to inform Mr. Bullock that word had just been
received from Tokyo tha all arrangements had been made for the
Australians to inspect the Japanese sodium anhydride process.
Mr. Allison then went on to say thet he wished to discuss
the proposed policy decision which the Australian representative
had made to the Far Eastern Commission in regard to the labor
situation in Japen -- specifically that the earlier FEC policy
C
S
couched in general terms was applicable to workers in Japanese
A
Government enterprises. Mr. Allison said tht we were all
disturbed over the labor situation in Japen; thet the Assistant
Secretery of Labor, Mr. Gibson, had recently made a trip to
Japan to look over the situation; tht he had found that at
least 60 per cent of the top leadershi in the trade union move-
ment in Japan was Communist or Communist-controlled; that there
was growing tenseness in the siturtion in Jepan, both political
ana economic; and th:t while we recognized fully that there
were many different ways of handling this question and t at it
was handled differently by different countries we must rely
upon the authorities on the spot to assess and evaluate the
situation with which they are faced. Mr. Allison explained
at some
C
CONFIDENTIAL
145721

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIAL
- 2 -
at some length that we appreciated the good motives which lay
back of the Australian proposal but that nevertheless if such a
proposal were adopted it would be a repudiation of SCAP's action
and a serious derogation of his authority and prestige which
the United States could not allow to happen. Accordingly, if
the Australien proposal were to be placed before the Commission
for a vote the United States would be impelled to vote against
it because of the highly serious and unfavorable reperoussions
such as increased labor strife, perhaps leading to riot and
civil disturbance, which would follow.
Mr. Allison added that the United States was concerned
equally with all other countries in the development of a sound
trade union movement in Japen. The United States, as were per-
haps other countries, was making known its views to the Supreme
Commander. It is our hope that the situation in Japan can be
alleviated through appropriate but unspectacular changes in
the Japanese laws and regulations without action by the Far
Eastern Commission.
Mr. Bullock said that his and like-minded governments had
been embarrassed by the position taken and the remarks made
by the Soviet member. In meking their proposal they had sought
merely to offset the Soviet proposals, some of which they quite
naturally supported, by presenting as innocuous a statement as
possible; thet the Australian Government would undoubtedly want
to give the most serious consideration to the probable reper-
cussions which the United States felt would follow from a policy
decision such as they had prepared; and that he was confident
that the Australian Government would not want to do anything
to derogate the prestige and authority of SCAP.
Mr. Allison re-emphasized that the United States shared
the concern of other governments nd recognized the particular
interest of such governments as Australia and Great Britain
which are under control of labor parties and that we would con-
tinue to welcome any suggestions which those governments might
have to make. Mr. Allison said that it had just occurred to
him that something in the way of a "consultative message" might
be worked out as a possible solution to the difficulties in
the Far Eastern Commission.
In conclusion Mr. Allison expressed the hope that we could
avoid the embarrassment of having to vote ageinst the Australian
proposal in the Far Eastern Commission and that we could reach
agreement among ourselves on some solution which would not
be apt to bring the undesirable repercussions in Japan which
we felt would follow if the Australian proposal were adopted.

Dear Mr. Butterworth:
894.504/5-
Following discussion with members of your staff of the
Australian resolution now before the Steering Committee of the
Far Eastern Commission concerning workers in Japanese government
enterprises, we dispatched a cable (WAR 87868) on 28 April to
The Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers. This cable outlined
a proposed U.S. course of action designed to deal with the
Australian resolution and requested the views of SCAP as to the
appropriateness of the steps set forth in the proposal.
We have now received a reply (C-69705, dated 1 May) in which
ScAP disagrees with the proposal on the grounds that under con-
49
ditions obtaining in Japan, the reaction to the adoption of this
proposed course of action would result in a serious threat to the
security of the occupation forces.
The Department of the Army subscribes to the position taken
by SCAP, namely that the United States should make known to friendly
members of the Far Eastern Commission its decision firmly to adhere
to the position already taken of supporting SCAp in this matter.
In my opinion, the U.S. should endeavor to secure the support of
these governments to the withdrawal or tabling of the Australian
proposal in order to avoid use of the veto power. Quite likely,
CS/
the friendly FEC nations do not appreciate the importance of the
FILED
14:949
points raised by SCAP in his latest cable. However, if it is no
possible to obtain the withdrawal or tabling of this resolution,
A
the Department of the Army believes that the U.S. should not
hesitate to use the veto in this case.
Very sincerely yours,
foot lest
ROBERT R. WEST
Deputy to
Assistant Secretary of the Army

SUBJECT:

New non-Communist Labor International.

Ir. Philip B. Suilivan, ILS

6.8
1-1683
like Ross called me this morning and stated
that Tewson of the British TuC had telephoned
James Carey, the Secretary-treasurer of the CIO,
in regard to plans for the organizational meeting of
the New Free Trade Unions International which will be
held in Geneva in thijunct on with the meeting of the
ILo. Tewson requested information regarding the
advisability of extending invitations to Japanese
and Korean trade unions. Specifically, Tewson asked
(1) are there any trade unions in those two countries
of such a nature that they should be invited, and
(2) if invited, would they be able to attend?
I told Ross that as far as Korea was concerned,
there was no organization of sufficient stature to
warrant an invitation. That, on the other hand, the
right-wing Japanese unions were not only worthy of
being invited but that such contacts as their repre-
sentatives would make at the meetings in question
would be extremely valuable for them in their present
conflict with the Communist-dominated left-wing groups
in Japan. I stated, however, that General MacArthur
had not permitted any Japanese trade unionists, of

-2-

invitation were sent to the trade unions in Japan to
attend the organizational meeting of the new non-
Communist international that official permisslon, in
all likelihood, would not be granted. Ross thereupon
inquired whether a Japanese worker would attend the

all probabilities one would be there, he stated that
they would make contacts with the workers' repre-
sentative at Geneva and that, "if necessary",
George Meany and Jim Carey would "kidnap" him for
informal attendance at some of the meetings for the
planning of the new international.

OFFICE OF

: ITP - Mr. Martin
INTERNATI TRADE POLLCY
D4TE: May 13, 1949
JUN 1 3 1949
FROM : IIS - Mr. Swayze
sujeCt: Labor Policy for Japan.
DED OF STATE
I. The report of Assistant Secretary of Labor
John W. Gibson (Attachment No. 1) following his trip to
Japan, copies of which have been officially transmitted
to both State and Army Departments as well as to General
LacArthur personally, stressos the importance of a sound
trade union movement to the achievement of the purposes of
our occupation of that country, and the need for adopt-
ing proper measures for retarding the threatening Com-
nmunist upsurge within the trade unions. His recommend-
ations come at a particularly significant time since a
series of recent developments renders a re-examination
and realignment of some aspects of our labor policy in
Japan essontial.
The more important of the measures recommended in
8
Mr. Gibson's report are:
(1) Limited revision of the National Public Service
4.50
Law in order to (a) restrict its coverage to bona fide
civil servants and (b) eliminate the penal provisions
for trade union activity;
(2) Limited revision of the general labor laws in
order to encourage democratic practices (such as
required use of secret ballots for election of
officers, annual elections, no officers on company
payrolls, etc.);
(3) Facilitating closer relations between U.S. and
Japanese labor leaders in order to give the latter
the benefit of U.S. labor's experience in developing
democratic unions and resisting minority control by
(a) sending "immediately" a committee of prominent
trade union leaders from the U.S. to Japan (as pro-
posed by Secretary Tobin) and (b) arranging for
Japanese unionists to come here for brief periods of
intensive study of U.S. unions.
(4) Provision for some flexibility in wage controls
under the Economic Stabilization Program where careful
study in given situations indicates serious hardships
or inequities exist.
II. (A) On
SECRET

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-2-
II. (A). On April 28 a joint State-Labor-Army wire
(Attachment No. 2) was dispatched to SCAP requesting his
views concerning the giving of the telecommunications
workers, the largest block of non-civil servants whose
labor relations are controlled by the provisions of the
National Public Service Law, the benefits of the more
liberal labor provisions of the Public Corporations Labor
Relations Law. Under this latter law employees are per-
mitted to organize, bargain collectively, utilize existing
mediation and arbitration machinery, but not to strike.
The desirability, as a matter of principle, of granting
greater labor rights to all non-civil-service employees of
the Japanese Government than are given to the civil servants
under the National Public Service Law has been expressed
to SCAP by State and Labor on several previous occasions.
Mr. Gibson also discussed this matter with General
MacArthur, who asked for specific recommendations for re-
vision of the Law in question. Mr. Gibson's report, as
noted above, makes this specific recommendation.
SCAP's reply of May 1 (Attachment No. 3) rejected the
idea, giving as his main reasons (1) the importance of un-
intorrupted communication facilities to security in Japan
(which is not impressive since no strike rights were pro-
posed but merely those of collective bargaining and
arbitration), and (2) that he would have to impose such a
change by force on the Yoshida Government. (See Attach-
ment No. 4 for more detailed analysis of SCAP's message.)
(B) Several very recent decisions by SCaP have the
effect of preventing any satisfactory development of the
third : lir. Gibson's recommendationsas listed above,
namely the establishment of satisfactory contacts between
Japanese trade unions and the non-Communist labor organi-
zations of the United States and other countries. In
early April he wired his disapproval of the projected visit
of a U.S. trade union mission to Japan on the grounds that
if such permission wore granted similar requests from other
countries, including the Soviet Union, could not be denied.
In keeping with this same policy, permission was also denied
to requests to permit Japanese workers to attend a non-
Communist Asian labor conference in India, and to permit-
ting the Japanese workers' observer to the recent ILo meeting
in Pittsburgh to attend a meeting of the United Mine Workers
to which he was invited by John L. Lewis. On much the same
grounds, that granting permission to one organization would
necessitate
SECRET

=== Page 185 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-3-
necessitate extending the same privilege to all other
requests of the same nature whatever their source, SCAP
on April 23 refused the request of the American Federation
of Labor to set up an office in Tokyo for purposes of
liaison with Japanese unions. It should be noted that the
offices set up by the AFL in Europe, including one in
Germany, have been extremely valuable adjuncts to U.S.
policy in Lurope, especially in combatting Communist efforts
among the trade unions there.
While it may be admitted that the giving of permission
for Japanese comunist labor leaders to go abroad is not
desirable per se, any more than is discriminating in such
matters in favor of one group of workers as compared to
another, nevertheless, the importance of broadening the
international contacts of non-conmunist Japanese labor
leaders is so great that neither of the two former alter-
natives appear sufficiently important to warrant blocking
the latter. Competent observers, without exception, report
that most intimate contacts exist between extreme leftist
Japanese labor leaders and members of the oversized Soviet
Hission in Tokyo. In view of this situation, any harm to
our interests that could come from allowing Japanese Com-
munists to go abroad to trade union meetings would fall
almost entirely into prestige and propaganda categories.
Hot letting then go has already furnished ample opportunity
for the latter of these, opportunity that has been well
utilized. Prestige and/or propaganda factors, however,
loom small when contrasted with the facts that on the staff
of the UsSR ission in Tokyo there are undoubtedly highly
trained snecialists in how minority groups operate to
capture and utilize for their purposes unions with pre-
dominately non-Communist membersships, while we do not have
a single U.S. trade unionist working with Japanese labor
today.
(c) From the foregoing, it is clear that no amount of
persuasion by the State and Labor Departments will succeed
in gaining SCaP acceptance of the very heart of the recom-
mendations proposed by r. Gibson. The recent negotiations
with Army on the telecommunications workers' case make it
likewise clear that Army will not support State on an issue
opposed by their Commander in the field.
III. It is my belief that steps looking toward the
achievement of the following ends should be undertaken at
once. They need to be undertaken because we are in danger
of
SECRET

=== Page 186 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-4-
of losing to the USSR and the Japanese Communists the battle
for Japanese trade union support of our occupation goals.
If the trade unions go Communist, which they need not do
because only a very small percentage of the organized
workers of Japan are Communist today, the end of the
occupation will see one of two things, Japan falling into
the Soviet orbit, or a reactionary right-wing government
smashing the trade union movement completely in the course
of which it will of necessity move further and further to
the right towards another type of totalitarian dictatorship:
(a) Amendment of the National Public Service Law by
(i) limiting its applicability to those government
employees who may rightfully be classified as civil
servants or vhose activities relate directly to the
maintenance of public security; and (ii) removing
the penal provisions of the law as they apply to
labor activities of government employees that are
not criminal per se.
(b) Actively encouraging contacts between Japanese
and non-Communist labor organizations of the U.S.,
Europe and Asia. This would involve (i) encouraging
rather than barring visits of U.S. and other labor
leaders to Japan, and (ii) permitting Japanese union
officials to go abroad for conferences and contacts
with labor organizations in democratic countries.
(c) Strengthening the SCAP Headquarters staff of
labor specialists by (i) assigning to high responsi-
bility a labor adviser familiar with U.S. labor
practices and capable of giving practical advice to
Japanese labor leadership in the development of a
non-Communist labor movement, and (ii) appointing a
number of experienced labor relations men directly
to the Labor Relations Branch of the Labor Division;
(iii) appointment of a highly qualified cost of living
and wage analyst to that section of the Headquarters
staff responsible for advising on the economic
stabilization program.
In view of the known positions of Army and SCaP, these
steps, in all probability, will require direct high level
advice to General acarthur which necessitates aligning
the interested cabinet officers behind a unified U. S.
Government position. Our immediate course of action
would
SECRET

=== Page 187 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-5-
would appear to be that of securing the support of
Mr. Thorp for the program outlined above with the view
of its being brought before the Under Secretary's Staff
Committee. In view of the similarity of this program
to lir. Gibson's proposals, we may count upon Lebor
Department support in such an undertaking.

(1) Mr. Gibson's report.
(2) Outgoing cable to General HacArthur regarding
proposed rights for government industrial workers;
(3) Reply from General HacArthur to (2) above.
(4) Analysis of (3) above.

The U.S. is confident that, working in this way, appropriate ad-

It seeme to me to be unwise to raise at the Cabinet

Therefore, I cannot support the first recommendation in
C0
the attaohed draft memorandum from you to Mr. Rusk.
6
94.504/6
I do not share the feeling that this problem "is
seriously embarrassing United States relations with
friendly FEC countries". To me that statement 1s a
great exaggeration. These FEC countries have not been
required to face up to the realities - military and
economic - of the situation in Asia. They approach
these problems with an academic detachment which has
1
9
characterized much of the basic post-war policies toward
649
Japan.

particular problem is "provoking antagonism to our

in that movement are Communists or fellow travellers and
S
will oppose us no matter what they may find opportune to
1
CVI

E
perhaps to ourselves for occasional over-emphasis on
"rights" without explanation of accompanying "respons-
ibilities". Mr. MacDiarmid has reported that a few
"defeats" for labor in Japan would be a healthy develop-
ment for the labor movement itself.
In view of the importance of internal security in
Japan to our future policies and programs, I would
recommend that we follow the course suggested in your
earlier memorandum to Mr. Rusk -- that he call in the
Australian Ambassador and urge that the resolution be
withdrawn or tabled. I would also recommend that he or
you call in the other friendly FEC Ambassadors and discusa
89
the position of Japan in the economic, political and
military security of Asia, and urge them to give full
support to U.S. policies. If the Australian resolution
is brought to a vote, I would recommend that we veto it.
I doubt that any serious propaganda repercussions would
follow. Our record of support of the labor movement in
7
Japan is, after all, a etrong one.

Subject: Japanes Supreme Court Labor DedaydtoNAL Tt POLICy

894.504/6-11
Sir:
LABOR
NLRB
I have the honor to refer to this Mission's despatch no. 193
of March 31, 1949, regarding the status of the Supreme Court of
Japan and to submit a summary of the first important decision by
the Court on a labor issue. In connection with this case there
11
are enclosed in translation a copy of an excerpt from the decision
of the Hiroshima Higher Court summarizing the facts of the case,
C
2/
a summary of the points made by the defendant-appellant on appeal
3/
to the Supreme Court of Japan, and a copy of the decision of the
4/
Supreme Court. A mimeographed copy of the Supreme Court's decision
in Japanese is also enclosed.
The facts of the case are briefly these: the miners union of
the Ohama Mining Company, Ltd., at Onoda, Yamaguchi Prefecture, pre-
sented a demand to the president of the company. Goroku SHIGEMORI,
for higher wages. This demand was refused and after preliminary
declarations in accordance with Japanese law the union began a
strike on September 10, 1947. The office employees union of the
company was split, the majority supporting the president of the
company and fourteen members supporting the miners union. After
negotiation the strike ended and the workers resumed work on Octo-
ber 5, 1947. On October 26, 1947, Shigemori suspended 78 members
of the union and on November 5, 1947, discharged 76 of them; the
two others and the fourteen members of the office employees union
were suspended.
The discharged union members caused a criminal action to be
brought against Shigemori on the ground that his actions were in
violation of Japanese labor laws providing that an employer must
not inflict injurious treatment upon or discharge laborers for en-
gaging in a work dispute or for making statements while engaged in
a dispute. Shigemori was found guilty by the Yamaguchi District
Court and fined 500 yen. Upon appeal the Hiroshima Higher Court
upheld the verdict and altered the sentence to two months imprison-
ment with sentence suspended for three years. Shigemori sppealed
to the Supreme Court.
The principaly
CC
UNCLASSIFIED
ACTION COPY
RETURN TO DC/R FILES WITHIN 14 DAYS, WITH A NOTATION OF ACTION TAKEN.

=== Page 207 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's despatch no. 374,
June 11, 1949.
-2-
The principal contentions of Shigemori were that the dispute
had previously been settled and that his actions in discharging 76
employees resulted from their "sabotage" (by which is apparently
meant their slow-down tactics after the dispute ended): that Shigemori
in suspending the fourteen office employees union members was justi-
fied since the contract with the office employees union called for a
closed shop and since the other members of this union had requested
the discharge of these workers; that the chief purpose of the dispute
was the erpulsion of President Shigemori from his position and was
not a legitimate labor union purpose; that suspension of the 76 em-
ployees did not constitute injurious treatment since the workers re-
ceived basic salaries while they were suspended; and that the original
decision of the Yamaguchi District Court made no finding that the dis-
charged amployees were responsible for sabotage and consequent damage
of the company's financial prospects.
On April 23, 1949, the Supreme Court dismissed Shigemori's appeal.
The Court's principal holdings are as follows: (1) the injurious
treatment of the workers by the employer was part of a causal chain
originating with the lawful dispute undertaken by the workers and was
not inflicted solely as a result of the alleged "illegal sabotage" of
the workers; (2) although a closed shop agreement existed between the
employer and the office employees union, it can not be readily assumed
that discharge of members of this union whose expulsion was desired by
other members of the union does not violate labor law provisions re-
quiring that an employer not inflict injurious treatment on workers
as a result of a labor dispute; (3) it can not be corluded that, where
a union asks for the dismissal of the chief of a mining office, the
purpose of a dispute involving that individual is not a legal one de-
signed to improve union conditions: (4) "injurious treatment" means
not only discriminatory treatment economically but also discriminatory
spiritual treatment such as suspension from work even with pay. The
Supreme Court upheld the decision of the lower court that the actions
of the employer brought pressure to bear on union members, and at
tempted to weeken and destroy the unity of the unions. The Court
made no specific finding that the union members had been guilty of
Babotage.
Although the sentence imposed on Shigemori was nominal. it is
considered that the Supreme Court has established an important prece-
dent in defining the right of an employer to discharge employees en-
gaged in a dispute and in actively opposing the management of the em-
ployer. The decision appears to be a liberal one, but it remains for
subsequent interpretations and later issues to determine the extent
of its application.

EXCERPT FROM DECISION OF THE HIROSHIMA HIGHER COURT
DECISION
Name:
Goroku SHIGMORI.
President, Ohama Tanko Kabushiki Kai sha
(Ohama Coal Mining Co., Ltd.), and
concurrently chief of Ohama Mining
Office.
Address:
1020 Onoda, Onoda Shi.

FACTS OF CRIHE (From decision of the Hiroshima Higher Court
dated May 21, 1948)
The accused is the chief of the Ohama Mining Office of the Ohama
Mining Co., Ltd., located at 1,O20 Onoda, Onoda. At this Mining Of-
fice there is a miners' labor union entitled "KOIN RODO KUMIAI" with
approximately 950 members and an office workers' union known as
"SHOKUIN RODO KUMIAI" consisting of eighty-odd office employees.
On August 20, 1947, there was a management conference, at which
the Miners Union submitted economic demands such as increased wages
under a sliding acale, etc. During the conference the accused re-
fused the demanded increase of wages under the sliding scale. His
speech and conduct subsequent to this refusal led to disputes, as a
result of which the accused declared the conference closed. There-
fore, ASAE Minsha, chairman, and all othor members of the Miners
Union passed a resolution on the following day, August 21, that the
accused, being undemocratic and despotic, should be expelled from
the Mining Office. The Miners Union handed this resolution to the
acoused on August 29, and entered upon a Labor dispute. On Septem-
ber 10, 1947, all of the Miners Union members started a general
trike.
On the other hand the Office Imployees Union was split into
two rival factions. The majority of the members supported the
acoused, but SAto Itsuhiko and 13 other members of the Union sup-
ported the Miners Union, and handing to the accused a request for
his resignation participated in the general strike. Thus the dis-
pute was increased. However, as a result of negotiations from
September 26 to 30 in Tokyo, where the head office of the company
is located, a general agreement was arrived at, whereby it was
agreed that economic demands such as an increase of wages under
a sliding scale and other details should be discussed and agreed
upon at the mine. The Miners Union, thereupon, withdrew the demand
for expulsion of the acoused, and discontinued the strike and re-
sumed work on October 5 of the same year.
However,
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure.No. 1,
Tokyo's Despatch No. 374,
June 11, 1949.
-2
However, the accused in the period between October 12 and 24
of the same year called to account some of the workers responsible.
for the strike and sabotage /Translator's note - taieye, generalle
translated as "sabotage" but also meaning "slow down"/ on the ground
of lowering of efficiency, endangering management order, and damage
of production. From his desire to bring a pressure to bear upon the
Union, to destroy unity of the Union, and to weaken it, the accused
on October 25 at the above Mining Office ordered suspension of work
and domiciliary confinement of chairman ASAE Minsha and 77 members
of the Union on the ground of responsibility for the sabotage. Not
only these members but also other members who were in the leading posi-
tions of the Unions and who were more. enthusiastic in the strike, were
given similar disciplinary treatment. He thus caused a decrease in
wages to the disadvantage of the miners.
On November 5, 1947, at the Mining Office the accused on the same
grounds as stated above discharged Asae Minsha and 75 members (except-
ing two of those who were ordered suspended).
On October 25, 1947, at the Kining Office the accused called to
account Sato Itsuhiko and 13 members of the Office Employees Union
for participation in the dispute in cooperation with the Miners Union
members, and ordered suspension of their work and domiciliary confine
ment until further notice. He thus caused a decrease in allowances to
the disadvantage of the employees.
The action of the accused in ordering suspension of work of the
said Miners Union members and office employees is considered to be
continued criminal intent.

SUMMARY OF APPEAL BY ATTORNEYS KEUKE NobuO and
HIROSAWA Michihiko in behalf of Goroku SHIGEMORI.
1. First Point
The acoused admitted the fact that he ordered suspension of work
of the said union members and that he discharged them. However, the
accused denies that he resorted to the above action with such inten-
tion as is insisted in the indictment. The reason that the accused
was compelled to take such actions was that, in spite of the fact
that the dispute started was amica bly compromised and settled between
the company and the union, some of the union members committed sabotage
in an organised and colle ctive manner without any justifiable reasons.
The situation where factory discipline was considerably destroyed hav-
ing continued long, the company was unable to overlook this situation.
The company first ordered suspension of work of leaders considered to
be responsible for the sabotage and others who instigated it, 78 per-
sons in all, for the purpose of demanding their reflection. Subse-
quently he discharged 76 workmen of the above 78 persons. It is evi-
dent, therefore, from the statements of the accused at the first trial
and at the criginal trial found in the records of this case that he
contended that his action was not illegal in the least.
It is beyond doubt from interpretation that the provisions of
paragraph 1 of Article 1l of the Labor Union Law or of Article 40 of
the Labor Relations Adjustment Law forbid dismissal or other dis-
advantageous treatment exclusively for the reasons prescribed there-
in, and that such provisions do not prohibit dismissal or other dis-
advantageous treatment in cases where the employers find justifiable
grounds for such an action. Accordingly, if the original decision
recognizes that the whole of those punished and discharged in this
case were responsible for the illegal sabotage, then the action of
the accused in blaming them for the illegal sabotage and in punish-
ing them by the suspension of work and discharge is legally justifiable.
Therefore, even if, for instance, the accused can be surmised to have
had the potential intent as is recognized by the original decision, to
search such a potential intent and forcibly render the accused's action
as in violation of the above described provisions of the laws must be
said to be an improper recognition in departure from the spirit of the
provisions of the laws. In short, in spite of the fact that the orig-
inal decision recognizes that some of the union members illegally sabo-
taged without reasons after the sattlement of the dispute and that the
extent of the sabotage was such that the management discipline of the
company's enterprise was destroyed and that the enterprise was in dan-
ger of eventual ruin. the original decision concluded that the accused
was guilty and used such ambiguous expression as "the said suspension
of work and diemissal of workers must be said as a whole to be illegal".
The original decision, in passing the conclusion, failed to give any
definite judgment as to whether or net each of the union members sue-
pended frem werk and discharged was respensible for the sabetage. The
original decision has thus either misinterpreted laws and made impreper
recegnition of facts er illegally lacked exhaustive investigatiens and
completeness of reasens. It is believed, therefore, that the original
decision must be repealed.
2. Second Point:
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2,
Tokyo's Despatch No. 374,
June 11, 1949.
-2-
2. Second Point:
The original trial should have first of all investigated as to
whether or not the so-called leaders of the Union had to be held
responsible for the illegal sabotage alleged by the company. Is it
has been determined that these persons included some who should be
held responsible for the sabotage, the accused should not be pre-
sumed to have had the intent to inflict illegal treatment. Iven
though the persons discharged included a large mumber of the leaders,
if all of the leaders had to assume responsibility for the sabotage,
it is a matter of course that they should be discharged as responsible
for the sabotage. Therefore, the conclusion that, merely for reasons
of the large number, the discharge contemplated other purposes--re-
sponsibility for the dispute, supression of the union, destruction
of the unity of the union, and weakening thereof--is a gap in reason-
ing, and cannot be said to be proper in the least. A discharge at
one time of a large mumber of leaders may destroy the unity of a
union and weaken it. However, this is a result, not a purpose. The
original decision has somewhat misunderstood the real intention of
the accused. The accused could, if he so desired, from the beginning
discharge the employees. For purpose of care and prudence in handling
the case, however, the accused first ordered suspension of work followed
by the discharge. It is not that the accused discharged them because
they admitted responsibility for the sabotage. It can be recognized
from reports of officers and men in charge that those discharged were
responsible for the sabotage. The accused considered it appropriate,
however, to give an opportunity for explanation for purpose of cir-
cumspection in handling the case, and at first ordered the suspension
of work. The accused expected the employees to make reflection and
submit an explanation. But in the absence of any attitude on the part
of the employees to make reflection, the accused announced the dis-
charge ten days after the order of the suspension of work.
The original decision is illegal in inconsistency of reasons
in concluding, on the basis of such dogma or surmise, that the pri-
marily lawful and proper suspension of work and discharge "are il-
legal as a whole". In this respect too the original decision is
believed to be deserving a repeal.
3. Third Point8
The original decision concludes as Fact No. 2 that "the accused
on October 25, 1947, at the said Mining Office called to account
office employees Sato Istuhiko and 13 others for participation in
the dispute in cooperation with the members of the Miners' Union,
ordered domiciliary confirement until further notice and suspension
of work, thereby caused a decrease in allowances, and thus imposed
injurious treatment". The order given to the employees to wait further
order at their homes in order to arrive at an amicable settlement of
the situation cannct be in violation of the provisions of the law. The
original decision has not given any judgment concerning this clear fact
insisted upon by the accused. In short the original decision is be-
lieved to be illegal in that it omitted a judgment concerning important
facts.
The original
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 213 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. 2.
Tokyo's Despatch No. 374,
June 11, 1949.
- 3 -
The original decision, as a result of overlooking the fact that
the said 14 persons are members of the Office Employees Labor Union,
has erred in the application of law and is therefore illegal. In
this point too the original decision is believed to be deserving a
repeal.
4. Fourth Point:
In its explanation of evidences and reasons the original decision
states as follows:
"In considering the circumstances of the dispute recognized
above, the said dispute involved economic demand from the begin-
ning to the end, and expulsion of the accused wes not its chief
aim. Accordingly, the said dispute has not deviated from the
regular scope of a labor union movement or a labor movement."
This statement of the original decision has either completely
ignored the evidences or has wilfully twisted the purport of the
evidences. Therefore, such recognition is illegal. According to the
evidence. what the Miners Union demanded of the company was from the
beginning to end the expulsion from office and elimination of the ac-
cused who was the chief of the Mining Office on the ground that the
accused was undemocratic and despotic.
The criginal decision states that the said dispute has not deviated
from the regular scope of the labor union movement or labor movement.
and treats this fact as a basic material for recognition of facts de-
scribed in the foregoing Second Point. Therefore, the original decision
in short has made a grave misunderstanding of facts concerning items
affecting the result of the decision. In this respect also the original
decison is believed to be deserving a repeal.
5. Fifth_Point
Article 40 of the Labor Relations Adjustment Law provides:
"In case an employer adjusts a labor dispute under this law,
he must not discharge the laborers involved or accord them in-
jurious treatment on the ground of such laborers' utterances or
dispute; provided, however, that a case where labor committee's
consent is obtained is excepted."
Thus the so-called illegal treatment on the ground of laborers'
dispute is forbidden. "Injurious treatment" is interpreted to mean
treatment reducing wages of involved laborers and otherwise lower-
ing economic advantages and labor conditions. However, this is in
short a judgment of value. Injurious treatment means only cases where
a difference between economic profits thus far acquired and damages
suffered from the treatment involved is, from ordinary social concept.
such as may be properly interpreted a disadvantage to the person or
persons involved. Therefore, a perfunctory interpretation that any
reduction of profits even to a very slight extent falls under the *in-
jurious treatment" prescribed in the Labor Relations Adjustment Law
is a misunderstanding of the spirit of the law.
The original
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 214 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2,
Tokyo's Despatch No. 374,
June 11, 1949.
The original decision states that the accused reduced the wages
of the Miners' Union membera by suspending the work of Asai Minsha
and other 77 members of the said Miners' Union, and that he thus in-
flicted injurious treatment. On the basis of such ambiguous facts
it is impossible to conclude whether the accused's treatment falls
under the "injurious treetment" mentioned in the Labor Relations Ad-
justment Law. Therefore, the original trial is improper in lacking
exhaustive investigations. If the original trial had interpreted
that any extremely small decrease in wages or allowances comes under
the "injurious treatment" prescribed in the Labor Relations Adjust
ment Law. this is a completo disinterpretation of the law. In this
respect the original decision is believed to be illegal and deserving
a repeal.
6. Sixth Point:
The original decision recognizes that the sabotage by the Miners'
Union members in this case was so strong as "to have lowered the effi-
ciency of work, destroyed management order, and endangered eventual
destruction of the enterprise". This mbotage was not merely a sabo-
tage of an individual member of the Union, but wes an organised and
collective one contering around miners and diggers. Seeing that the
sabotage seemed to continue indefinitely the company demanded chair-
man Asae and a few other leaders of the Union to discontinue the
illegal sabotage immediately. However, the chairman and other leaders
of the Union replied that the union had not ordered the sabotage. The
union thus ignored the company's demand, and showed an attitude that
they tacitly approved the sabotage.
It was intolerable for the company to overlook indefinitely such
sabotage, improper and without any reasons. The financial conditions
of the company at that time showed considerable losses on account of
the strike of the miners for a long time. and further losses from the
sabotage made it difficult for the company to continue bus iness. At
tha time the company barely maintained its finance by the aid of the
"defieit" financial accommodations, and further losses were liable to
render the maintenance and continuation of the enterprise difficult.
The company's management (Managing Director TAkAHASHI and Assistant
Chief KunIna), which was patient in dealing with the Union at the spot.
had to finally make up its mind to take a drastic action to have the
sabotage discontinued. The accused left the Mining Office on October
8 for Oeaka and Tokyo to seek financial accommodations, and was not
at all aware of the sabotage started from October 12. He received a
detailed report on the sabotage from Chief Secretary NAGAI Kosaku of
the Office Employees Labor Union who met him in the trip. Greatly
surprised the accused returned to the mine in a great hurry on Octo-
ber 23. Upon his return he immediately called a conference of the
company's erecutives including Managing Director Takahashi, Assistant
Chier Kunida. Chief of Labor Section HAITANI. etc. He also received
reports from officers in charge at the mine. Considering the situa-
tion precarious permitting no delay in action he, on October 25.
ordered suspension of work of 78 persons for responsibility for the
sabotage. On November 5 he discharged 76 persons out of the 78
persons. As a result of this drastic action the sabotage immediately
came to an
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 215 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. 2.
Tokyo's Despatch No. 374,
June 11, 1949.
- 5 -
came to an end. Thereafter, order was restored throughout the
mine, and efficiency of work has since been very satisfactorily
increasing.
The above circumstances have been made clear in the records of
this case. The action in suspending work and discharging employees
was taken as emergency and inevitable measures to save the company's
enterprise from the miners' sabotage, and is not illegal in the
least.
The accused had no other purpose than to have the sabotage dis-
continued. If he, as alleged by the original decision, suppressed
the union and discharged even innocent miners on the pretext of re-
sponsibility for the sabotage with the intent of destroying unity of
and weakening the unicn, then over 900 miners other than those dis-
charged could not have returned to work peacefully without any commo-
tion.
However, the original decision, without giving a definite judg-
ment as to whether or not those discharged were actually responsible
for the sabotage, concluded under a mere surmise that the accused had
the intent to suppress the union, basing such conclusion on the fact
that those discharged included a large number of the union leaders.
etc. That such a conclusion is improper has been already described.
The criginal decision fatls to grasp such clear reason, and had
rendered the conclusion described in the foregoing in such a tone and
ambiguous manner that the union leaderst responsibility for the sabo-
tage was not recognized. The original decision has either misinter-
preted the law or laws. or is illegal in that investigations were in-
complete and that there are omissions in the judgment. For these
reasons the original decision is believed to be deserving a repeal.

DECISION OF SUPRELE COURT
Name of the Accused: Gorcku SHIGEMORI,

The accused has appealed to this Supreme Court the decison of
the Hiroshima Higher Cour: delivered on May 21, 1948, concerning
the case of vicletions by the accused of the Labor Union Law and
Labor Relations Adjustment Law. This Supreme Court has reached the
following decision
This appeal is hereby diemissed.
REASONS
1. Fat pe ef ts appeal

As in ndicated in the lecixion. where the employer called

and effect between on the one han! the fact that the laborers are mem-
bere of a labor unlon, that the laborere committed lawful acts as mem-
bers of a laber union, and that laberers resorted to dispute, and on
the other hand the injurious treatment against the laborers, the em-
ployer's injurious treatment against the laborers is considered to
be in violation of the provisions of Article ll of the Labor Union
Law or
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 217 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 3,
Tokyo's Despatch No. 374,
June 11, 1949.
-2-
Law or Article 40 of the Lebor Relations Adjustment Law. Therefore.
the origimal decision that the accused violated each of the above
provisions has not in the least misinterpreted the law and is not
illegal. Also in the original decision there is no such illegality
as is pointed out in the attorney's arguments es to insufficient
reasons or imcompleteness of examinations.
2. Second point of the appeal.
The criminal acts recognized by the original decision are fully
substantiated by evidence shown in the original decision. There are
found no points of illegality. as are alleged, such as recognition
of facts without evidence or violation of law concerning procurement
of evidence. The attorneys' arguments insist in detail that the in-
jurious treatment of the Labor Union members by the accused had the
purpose of punishing only the illegal sabotage of the union members
as is stated in the original decision. However, the original deci-
sion, as explained in the first point, dces not deny that the accused
had the intention of blaming some of the union members for illegal
sabotage. The original decision recognizes that the injurious treat
ment of the union members not only punished this illegal sabotage
but also intended to call them to account for the dispute. The orig-
inal decision recognized that for other members of the Union reaponsi-
bility for dispute was molely meant. Therefore. the attorneys' argu-
ments merely stress facts not found in the original trial. In short
the arguments insist on erroneous finding of facts in the original
trial. and therefore the arguments do not constitute lawful reasons
for appeal.
3. Third point of the appeal.
There was concluded a labor contract in November 1946 between
the Ohama Coal Mining Company's Mining Office and the Ohama Coal
Mine Employees Labcr Union organized by office employees of the said
Mining Office, according to the evidence. It is also found from the
evidence that the contract provided for the so-celled closed shop
system, that SATo Iteuriko and 13 other of the employees union were
expelled from the unior twice in about the latter part of September
1947 and about the first part in October of that year, and that the
union demanded the compary to discharge those who had been expelled.
As is pointed out in the attorneys' arguments, in cases where
a closed shep system is provided for in a labor contract concluded
between an employer and a labor union and the union has expelled a
member therefrom, it is true that the employer in the abeence of
special circumstances is obligated to discharge the expelled member.
However, even in a case where a closed shop system is provided for.
a discharge of an expelled member by the employer or any other in-
jurious treatment cannot readily be assumed not to violate the pro-
visions of Article 40 of the Labor Relations Adjustment Law. Even
in such a case a thorough investigation should be made of the fol-
lowing: the contents of the agreement concerning the closed shop
eystam, the relatione between the labor union and the employer.
the reasons for exclusion of the union members, whether or not the
exclusion was made voluntarily by the union, and the intention of
the employer who imposed injurious treatment. It should be decided
whether or not
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 218 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. 3.
Tokyo's Despatch No. 374,
June 11, 1949.
-3-
whether or not the injurious treatment is in violation of the pro-
visions of Article 40 of the law on the basis of whether or not the
treatment illegally interferes with the right of dispute of the
laborers.
The fact that the original decision failed to explain the various
circumstances above described, and immediately decided that the in-
jurious treatment in this case is in violation of the Labor Relations
Adjustment Law raises criticism that the decision did not contain an
exhaustive explanation. According to the records of the case, however,
it can be surmised and concluded that the original trial made thorough
study and investigation of the above described circumstances. The orig-
inal decision has expecielly concluded, on the basis of depositions of
witnes SATo Itsuhiko, that the expulsion of the said individual and
13 other persons by the Office Employees Labor Union had not been made
voluntarily by the said union. It may be surmised and recognized that
the original decision has eventually come to the conclusion that, re-
gardless of the circumstances such as the closed shop system and the
expulsion, the injurious treatment in this case is in violation of the
provisions of the law. The foregoing point concerning the closed shop
Bystem does not come under the provisicne "Reasons preventing constitu-
tion of a crime from the legai etandpoint" stipuleted in paragraph 2 of
Article 360. old Code of Criminal Procedures. Therefore. the onginal
decision, althcugh laciing a specific mention of conclusions, cannot
be necessarily concluded to be illegal as alleged.
4. Fourth point of the apneal.
Even when in the labor dispute demand was made for expulsion of
the chief of the Chama Mining Office of the Ohama Coal Mining Company.
Limited. it can not be concluded that this was the direct and exclusive
purpose but rather that such an act. as a necessary means to obtain
maintenance and improvement of laber conditions. does not necessarily
depart from the lawful extent of labor union activities.
The original decislon concluded that in this dispute the union
members ineisted from the beginning to the end on economie demands
such as increased wages under a sliding scale, and had not as its
chief aim the expulsion of the accused, chief of the Mining Office.
Moreover. since this fact can be perceived from the circumstances of
the dispute the original decision concluded that the dispute, as a
labor union movement or a lator movement, had not deviated from a
regular sphere and was not illegal in the least. The points of argu-
ments merely argue the facts already decided by the original decision
to the effect that the insistence of the union members was from the
beginning to the end economic demand and that the expulsion of the
chief of the Mining Office indicated in the decision was not their
chief aim. Therefore. the arguments have no grounds.
5. Fifth point_of the eppeal.
The injurious treatment referred to in Article il of the Labor
Union Law or Article 40 of the Labor Relations Adjustment Law should
be interpreted not only to mean injurious and discriminatory economic
treatment
UNCLASS IFIED

=== Page 219 === (confidence 0.98)
Enelosure No. 3,
Tokyo's Despatch 374,
June 11, 1949.
- 4-
treatment such as reduction of salaries, suspension of raise of
salaries, etc., but also to include broadly injurious and discrimina-
tory spiritual treatment. Accordingly, when an employer has ordered
suspension of work, it may be interpreted to come under the so-called
injurious treatment prescribed in these provisions, although no in-
jurious result is brought about thereby in economic treatment such as
allowances, etc. Specifically, the accused ordered suspension of work
of ASAE Minsha and 77 other members of the union, according to the o-
riginal decision, and acknowledged that he thereby reduced allowances.
Also according to the evidence it can be concluded that the union
members were, as a result of the suspension of work, entitled to only
basic salaries, and not to family allowances or allowances for work
in the pits. Therefore, it is not illegal in the least that the o-
riginal decision interpreted the suspension of work to mean the injuri-
ous treatment prescribed in the foregoing provisions of law.
6. Sixth point of the appeal.
It is as explained in the preceding paragraphs thet in short the
original decision recognized that in the cases of ASAE Minsha and 70
odd members of the Mining Labor Union and SATo Itsuhiko and 13 members
of the Mining Office Ewployees Union respectively, the accused ordered
suspension of wcrk and discharge, blaming them for the dispute, bring-
ing pressure to bear upon the union members, and attempting to destroy
the unity of the unions and to weaken them. The argunents in the ap-
peal reiterate that the union members were responsible for the illegal
sabotage. and that the treatment in this ease was made to call to as-
count the eabotage. On these premises the arguments merely attack the
originel decision. In short the arguments criticize the finding of
facts which belongs to the exclusive right of the original trial. Ac-
cordingiy. the arguments do not constitute lawful grounds for appeal.
For the reasons above described, the above decision is given as
the text in acccrdance with the provisions of Article 2 of the Law
Governing Enforcement of the Code of Criminal Procedures and of Arti-
cle 446 of the old Code of Criminal Procedures.
The above is the unanimous opinion of all judges concerned.
Procurator MIYAMOTO Masuze participated in the decision.
April 23, 1949

KURIYAMA Shigeru
FUJITA Hachiro

STANDARD FORM NO. 64
CONFIDENTIAL
Office Memorandum
JUN 2 31949
TO
The Acting Secretary
DIVISION OF
TE: June 13, 1949
Through :
s/s
FE - Mr. Butterworth lbw
FROM
SUBJECT:
Your Meeting with the Australian Ambassador on June 14
Problem
To persuade the Australian Ambassador to table or
withdraw his Government's resolution now before the FEC
relating to the labor rights of Japanese Government
enterprise workers.
Discussion
89
Last summer, as a result of serious abuses of power
94.504/6-1349
by organized Japanese Government workers, SCAP recommended
and, under the direction of occupation officials, the
Japanese Government passed several laws, known as the
National Public Service Legislation, restricting the
rights of Japanese Government employees. The USSR and
the Japaneee Communists condemned SCAp's action in toto.
The FEC countries and United States organized labor have
criticized the restrictive provisions of these laws as
excessive. The United States Government (State, Army,
Labor) has informed SCAP privately that it regarded
certain phases of the laws defective and has unsuccess-
fully attempted over the last nine months to have certain
minor corrective measures taken. At the same time, the
United States Government has opposed the passage of any
FEC policy decision which would publicly reverse SCAP
on this highly delicate issue.
The issue presents itself today in the form of an
Australian Resolution before the FEC which provides that:
"Bearing in mind that it is a matter for the
occupation authority in Japan to decide when strikes
and other work stoppages should be prohibited as
directly prejudicing the objectives or needs of the
C
occupation, the FEC decides as a matter of policy
that the provisions of FEC O45/5 (principles for
175655
Japanese Trade Unions) should be applied to workers
A
in Japanese Government enterprises."
Despite repeated attempts by the United States Govern-
ment to have this resolution defeated or tabled, the Resofu-
tion was passed by the Steering Committee on May 31 by a
2
vote
31943
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 271 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIAL
- 2 -
vote of 6 to 1, the United States alone opposing and the USSR,
China, Netherlands and Philippines abstaining. It 1s now
before the Commission and will probably be voted upon within
the next few weeks.
The U.S. is therefore faced with an immediate choice
between three course of action: (1) not voting against
the resolution which, in effect, would permit its adoption.
This is highly inadvisable for it would directly reverse
SCAP, require a major rewriting of the National Publio
Service Legislation, unquestionably produce politioal
turmoil in Japan, etc. (2) Defeating the resolution by
a U.S. veto.  This would require that the U.S. use the
veto to defeat a resolution which no other countries
oppose. It would not settle the controverey but afford
the Soviets an opportunity publicly to present the U.S.
as alone opposed to the "rights" of Japanese labor. (3)
Further seeking the withdrawal or tabling of the resolution.
Although the Australian Delegation to the FEC is acting
under firm orders on this matter from the Australian Labor
Government, there is believed to be some merit in making a
high-level restatement of our position in the hope that the
Australian Ambassador will act at least to seek the post-
ponement of an FEC vote. In the meantime the Department
plans to make a further joint State-Army-Labor approach
to SCAP with a view to his having certain minor modifica-
tions made in the National Public Service Legislation,
which are designed to dispose of this problem in the FEC.
(The thought in the previous sentence should not, of course,
be communicated to the Australian Ambassador.)
Recommendation
It 1s recommended that you raise this question with the
Australian Ambassador, pointing out to him the desirability
of tabling or withdrawing his Government's resolution. There
follows some of the arguments that might be presented to him.
(1) The controlling FEC directive, to which
the Australian resolution refers, is a broad state-
ment of principles for Japanese trade unions, which
does not state to which specific group it does or
does not apply. SCAP acted within his proper admin-
istrative discretion in interpreting the direotive
aB
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 272 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
- 3 -
as he saw fit, and his interpretation of the
directive has been translated by the Japanese
Government into a series of laws. The effeot
of the Australian resolution, if adopted,
would be to require a major rewriting of these
laws which in turn would undermine the position
of the present Government, create political
unrest and jeopardize the stabilization program.
(2) The Australian resolution would permit
unrestricted use of the strike weapon by Govern-
ment enterprise workers unless expressly for-
bidden by military fiat. This would impose on
the occupying authority (and on the U.S. as
principle occupying power) the direct respons-
ibility for the banning of each strike as it
arose --- a situation which would create
continual uncertainty and turmoil.
(3) The adoption of the Australian resolu-
tion would be an FEC reversal of a position
publicly taken by SCAp. The loss of prestige
which SCAp would suffer from such a reversal
would seriously threaten the ocsupation. It
would play directly into the hands of the Com-
munists.
(4) Accordingly, if the Australian proposal
1s put to a vote, the U.S. will be impelled to
vote against it. The Australians will not gain
their objective, the U.S. will be placed in an
embarrassing position. No one, except the Soviets
and their Japanese Communist Party minions, will
reap any advantage from the vote.
(5) The U.S. regrets that on a question
bearing so directly upon Japan's internal security
the U.S. and Australia cannot see eye to eye. For
the U.S. has assumed the principle burden for
maintaining, on behalf of itself and of likeminded
powers, the peace and security of the Pacific.
The U.S. will be hard put to carry out this
responsibility if it does not have the support
of Australia and of other friendly powers on
matters directly affecting their common security.
66.3

FE - Mr. Butterworth
- Mr. Allison
May 4, 1949
NA - Mr. Bond
Exchange of Telegrams with SCAP on Labor Situation
You will be interested in the underlying exohange of
telegrams between State-Army-Labor on one hand and SCAp on
the other. SCAp's refusal to agree to the carefully reasoned
proposal set forth in the attached outgoing telegram--a
proposal which imposes no difficult or immediate obligation on
SCA P and yet which would serve to extricate the U.S. Govern-
ment from a highly embarrassing position--further illustrates
the importance of a fundamental re-evaluation of the existing
regime of control in Japan.
Discussed seriatim below are the arguments raised by SCAP:
(1) SCAP states that since the Government owns and
operates all communication facilities upon which the police
are completely dependent, the telecommunications workers should
be regarded as a security adjunct and placed under direct
Government control.
Comment:
If we were advocating that telecomminications workers
be given the right to strike, SCAP's arguments would
appear to be valid; but it is difficult to see how the
extension of themot mediation and arbitration rights
would entail national disaster. Furthermore, according
to DRF Information Note of February 8, 1949, the police
communications network has been "thoroughly reorganized
to improve the direct channels of contact between the
National rural police and the local police, allowing
direct communication from Tokyo Headquarters to the
smallest police unit".
(2) SCAP states that it is not clear why the United States
should hesitate if necessary to use the veto to defeat the
Australian proposal.
Comment:
As stated in our telegram, the exercise of a veto to
defeat the Australian proposal would be made known to the
press by the Soviet member, placing the U.S. Government
in a
SECRET

=== Page 285 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-2-
in a highly embarrassing position. The veto would not
solve the basic issue and the labor question would
continue to fester in the FEC. It is U.S. policy to
avoid unilateral exercise of the veto whenever possible.
(3) SCAP quotes at length the views of Commissioner
Frances Perkins on why we should not extend to Japanese Govern-
ment workers rights not enjoyed by the U.S. Government workers.
Comment:
If the U.S. Government were "in business" as is the
Japanese Government, it would be in a better position to
argue the applicability of its laws and customs to the
situation in Japan. Government enterprise workers in
the British Conmonwealth countries have full collective
bargaining rights.
(4) SCAP argues that the statutory changes which would
be mandatory under our proposal would have to be imposed "by
force" on the Yoshida Government.
Comment:
It is recalled that SCAP imposed last year on the
Socialist-Democratic coalition rogime a series of recom-
mendations respecting Government workers rights that were
far more extensive and considerably more repugnant to that
regime than the mere proposal, described by SCAP as a
"purely domestic detail", that a telecommunications
corporation be established. During the last month SCAp's
labor division has succeeded in persuading the Yoshida
Government to tone down considerably its Trade Union
Adjustments Act. There is no reason why any greater
pressure would be necessary for persuading the Yoshida
Government to support the creation of a Telecommunications
Corporation. But in any case, we made it explicit in our
telegram that SCAP should set up this corporation only
when practicable in the light of emergency conditions
peculiar to the situation in Japan.
(5) SCAP claims that to appear even partially to reverse
the position he has taken would lend immense support to false
Soviet pretense that it alone defends the rights and well-
teing of Japanese workers".
Comment:
It is probably true that the Soviets are giving the
impression to Japanese workers that the USSR is concerned for
the
SECRET

=== Page 286 === (confidence 1.00)
SECRET
-3-
the rights of workers. It is precisely for this reason
that we regard it important that the U.S. appear at
least equally concerned for the rights of Japanese
workers.
(6) SCAP maintains that the general trend of events in
the Far East "gives grave warning against any compromise of
principal at this tima".
Comment:
In the case of Gover ment enterprise workers SCAP
has enunciated no principle which we can defend or which
could be compromised.
(7) SCAP states: "It seems apparent that if friendly
governments would accept the proposal contained in para 2 of
part 5 urmsg, they would with no less alacrity yield to the
end desired if informed that the United States intended firmly
to adhere to its opposition to the changes provided in the
Australian proposal".
Comment:
The essential difference between our proposal and
SCAP's recomnended course of action is that our proposal
entails a clear statement of principle on the rights of
Japanese Government workers--a statement which the other
countries have repeatedly sought from us. Failure to
express our Government's views on this subject, as well
as failure to recognize the points of view expressed by
them in the FEC and elsewhere, will almost inevitably
result in ∞ntinued controversy over Japanese Government
workers rights. Because the Australian proposal offers
the right to strike to Government workers, we could in
all conscience present an effective argument against the
Australian proposal; but we would have, in my opinion, no
effective arguments against an FEC proposal that all
Government enterprise workers be given full collective
bargaining rights, but not the right to strike. It seems
quite probable that if the Australian proposal is with-
drawn or defeated, a resolution along the foregoing
lines would be introduced by one of the British Common-
wealth c ountries.
(8) SCAP claims that the Soviets would gain little satis-
faction from the American veto of the Australian proposal,
inasmuch as the Soviets are already on record as strongly
opposed thereto.
Comment:
SECRET

=== Page 287 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
Before making any final recommendations on how this matter
should now be handled, I believe it would be well for us to
sound out the reactions of Mr. West and Mr. Gibson. The Army
Department will undoubtedly support SCAP to the hilt and the
Labor Department will probably take a completely opposite
point of view. For the Labor Department, and for Mr. Gibson
in particular, SCAp's telegram is an unhappy sequel to his
telegrams rejecting the Labor Department's proposals for
sending a Labor Mission to Japan and for establishing an AFL
office in Tokyo.
Although SCAP has refused to go along with our proposal,
it is recommended that we proceed with the planned approaches
to friendly FEC countries in an effort to have the Australian
proposal tabled or withdrawn. If these approaches fail, the
Department is faced with the alternative of:
(1) Vetoing the Australian proposal, which would not
dispose of the overall labor issue and would expose
our Government to not inconsiderable embarrassment
and criticism;
(2)
Countering the Australian proposal with a U.S.
resolution or statement that, "at the earliest
moment deemed appropriate by General MacArthur,
government enterprise workers be given full
collective bargaining rights including mediation
and arbitration but not the right to strike"-
a resolution which would almost certainly be
acceptable to the friendly FEC countries and which
would probably dispose of the problem definitively.
The second alternative or any variation of it would be to
reverse SCAP. It would obviously be a course of action which
we would not pursue without agreement at a high level in this
Government.
In any event, it is suggested that this matter be brought
to the Secretary's attention as one more illustration of how
the situation in Japan has gotten out of hand and why plans
are now being formulated in FE for a change in the regime of
control and for a peace treaty.

DEPUTY UNBER SECRETARY OF STATE

TO
: G - Mr. Rusk
DATE: June 30, 1949
1R
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
FROM : FE - Mr. Butterworth olo
Fle
suBJect: Labor Rights of Japanese Government Enterprise Workers
I have been handed a copy of the draft memorandum to you
dated June 19 fron: Mr. Thorp on the subject "Australian Reso-
lution before the FEC on the Rights of Employees of Japanese
Government Enterprises". Since this is not an economic problem
but is directly concerned with one of the most delicate and
important political issues in our policy toward Japan and in
our relations with the occupation I wish to place FE's views
on record.
This Office egrees with E that the 3CAP-eponsored legis-
lation dealing with Government workers' labor rights is defect-
ive in certein respects and has attempted for the last ten
months to have minor corrective measures taken. At the same
time FE hes opoosed the passage of any FEC policy decision which
would publicly reverse SCAP, causing him to suffer a loss of
es
prestige.
8
Where this office cannot agree with Mr. Thorp is with re-
6
epect to his contention that a Us unilateral use of the veto
4
to defeat the Australian propoeal would have the "eerious dis-
advantage" of causing the US to be presented "by press and
5
radio as having alone opnosed the rights of Japanese labor".
04
In the several talks which members of this Office have had with
Australian Embassy officials on this subject, the latter have
come to recognize the validity of our position and have intimated
that they could see no reason why we should not seek to dispose
of their resolution by opposing it in the FEC. Meanwhile, the
N
Australians have not pressed for a vote on the resolution for
O
over a month now, which leads me to suspect that they may be
4
reconsidering their position in the light of current labor strife0
in Japan. The Australians and other friendly FEC countries have
assured us that they would not ventilate this matter outeide the
confines of the FEC. The Soviets may well do so, but, coming
from that quarter, it is believed that their claims will not
gain much credence outside circles sympathetic to the Communists.
In any case, we have ample ammunition to counter Soviet propa-
ganda attacks shculd they occur; for the liberal character of
our occupational labor policy is a subject toward which we need
have no apologies but is one which we would do well to proclaim
from the housetops. We cen meet eny propaganda challenge.

=== Page 291 === (confidence 0.95)
-2-
The other principal objection of this Office to Mr. Thorp'
memorandum concerns the advisability of raising the labor issue
with SCAP at this time. When last we did so, we received a
complete rebuff. Since then, a number of serious strikes and
strike threats have developed in which the very workers with
which we are concerned - those in the Japanese Government tele-
communications and railways - have assumed the most militant,
threatening posture of all. No one will dispute, I am sure,
that the next three months will witness a most critical period
in the occupation. As the stringent stabilization program goes
into effect we can expect intensive labor strife to develop,
backed by the Communists whose present primary objective is to
bring discredit to the US occupation. The wisdom of avoiding
any rocking of the boat during this period is apparent. From
all evidence at hand, it is obvious that SCAP will reject the
proposal set forth in the telegram apnended to Mr. Thorp's
memorandum.
I therefore, recommend that, for the present, this matter
not be taken up with Mr. Steelman or with Secretary Johnson
and that Mr. Thorp's telegram not be sent.

SECTION A

21st July, 1949
TRUD
CFFENSIVE OF REACTION AGAINST THE
JAPANESE WORKING CLASS
Dy Kh. Eidus
of a bitter class struggle. The American occupation authoritie
the reactionary government of Yoshida and Japanese monopoliats
are conducting a conoentrated offensive against the vital
interesta and trade union rights of the working people
The notorious American programme of
"economic stabilisation" was worked out for Japan in December
1948. The aim of this programme consisted in ensuring the
maximum profits from American capital which was beingeinvested
in Jppanese industry, that is,to say to increase the income
of the monopolies at the expense of the further lowering
of the standard of living of the working people.
"Stabilisation" began with the Japanese
Ministry of Labour distributing to the governors of the
Prefecturesa directive approved by MacArthur's staff on the
dissolution of those Japanese trade unions which were allegedly.
carrying on political activity. The Yoshida Government
with the approval of the American occupation authorities worked
out at the same time new reactionary bills further curtailing
the right of the workers and trade unions.
The new laws which in character recall
the American Taft-Hartley anti-workers! law in essence forbida
strikes not only in public but also in private undertakings.
The new anti-worker legislation is
the direct result of the Aherican programme of the "economic
stabilisation" of Japan.
The American programme has already
led to. a re w increase in taxation, a rise in prices, a lowering
of wages and the growth of unemployment.
The class strugglc in Japan assumes
every day yet sharper features. The polise more and more
frequently attack strikers, meetings and demonstrations of the
working people, crudely violating their rights and using physical
force.
The police terror is primarily directed
against the Japanese Comnunist Party which is the only political
Party in Japan struggling for genuine democracy and the
demilitarisation of Japan, for its freedom and independence against
reaction and
fascism, against the enslavement of the country
by American monopolistic capital. The constant growth of
the influence of the Japanese Communist Party is evoking the
wrath of the Ame rican/Japanese reactionarics.
On the 4th July this year MacArthur
made a special statement directed against Communism. He ended
his statement with the question: "How long will this movement
enjoy the protection of the law?", thereby hinting at the
desirability of declaring the Communist Party illegal. The
Japanese Minister without Portfolio, Higan, replied with the
statement that the government was preparing appropriate measures.
The attempts of the servants of the
American monopolists are doomed to failure. Even the reactionary
newspaper "Nippon Times" was recently compelled to admit-with:
contrition that a big movement to the left had taken place in:
the Japanese trade unions.
These changes are the result of the
growing class consciousness of the working people who in their
own experience have savoured the delights of American "democracy".

1549

THE WHitE House, August 27, 1949.
To the Senate of the United States:
With a view to receiving the advice and consent of the Senate to
ratification, I transmit herewith an authentic text of the convention
(No. 87) concerning freedom of association and protection of the
right to organize, adopted by the International Labor Conference at
its thirty-first session, held at San Francisco, June 17 to July 10, 1948.
I transmit also for the information of the Senate the report of the
Secretary of State regarding this convention, together with a copy of
a letter addressed by the Secretary of Labor to the Secretary of State
with respect to the convention.
HARRY S. TRUMAN.

letter from the Secretary of Labor, (3) authentie text of convention
(No. 87) concerning freedom of association and protection of the
